White House Has 'Repeatedly Violated' Court Order to Return Press Access, Say Three Media Outlets

TIME | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings CNN, MS NOW, and Politico filed an emergency request for a hearing Thursday morning, arguing that the White House has "repeatedly violated" a federal judge's order for President Donald Trump to immediately restore their press access. "This morning, journalists from each of CNN, MS NOW, and Politico attempted to enter the White House and were turned away, and Secret Service agents confiscated the hard pass of journalists whose hard passes had not previously been confiscated," the outlets said in the court filing. Politico said its reporter arrived at the White House campus only to be denied access and have their press credentials seized. CNN said one of its reporters and a photojournalist were denied access to the White House grounds, "despite multiple attempts." Meanwhile another CNN producer was able to enter the White House just after 6 a.m. ET. MS NOW relayed similar experiences, noting that while one of its reporters, alongside a producer, were denied entry and had their passes confiscated, another MS NOW producer was granted access. Judge Timothy Kelly has given the Trump Administration until 12:30 p.m. ET to respond to the outlets' filing. TIME has approached the White House and Justice Department for comment. Early Thursday, in a District of Columbia court, Judge Kelly issued a temporary restraining order of 14 days against the ban, saying it “likely violated” the media outlets’ “constitutional due process rights.” He ordered that journalists from the banned media outlets should have their White House access reinstated “until further order of the court or the expiration of this temporary restraining order.” In a statement to TIME, Ted Boutrous, the lawyer representing the news outlets, said “this is a strong ruling vindicating freedom of the press, due process and the rule of law. We greatly appreciate the court’s swift action.” The order came in response to a lawsuit filed against the Trump Administration by CNN, MS NOW, and Politico on Monday, in which the outlets argued the ban was a “direct assault” on the First Amendment and violated their Fifth Amendment due process rights. Outlining his ruling, Kelly said the outlets had “shown a likelihood of success, at least on their Fifth Amendment procedural due process claim.” The Fifth Amendment protects Americans from being “deprived of life, liberty, or property, without due process of law.” Kelly determined that the outlets were “likely to succeed in showing that their hard passes were revoked without constitutionally adequate due process.” The judge also rejected the Trump Administration's assertion that the media ​ban was motivated by “national security” concerns. “Certainly, that is not what President Trump said when he announced that he was ‘banning’ plaintiffs from the WhiteHouse—instead, he focused on the alleged lack of truthfulness and negativity of plaintiffs’ reporting,” Kelly wrote, referencing Trump’s “fake news” reasoning. The Justice Department, in a legal filing on Tuesday, said the outlets had “received adequate notice” of the ban (a point contested by Kelly) and insisted “the President’s actions are lawful under the First Amendment.” A deadline has been set for the Trump Administration to respond to the media outlets' request for an emergency hearing. Meanwhile, ahead of Thursday’s order, Trump dismissed the legal challenge over the ban and indicated he regretted appointing Judge Kelly in 2017. Vowing to fight any legal block on his ban, Trump insisted: “Almost without question and, as usual, we’ll go for appeal, because fake news people and publications that only write negatively, and who violate our national security by writing false and defamatory stories with unknown ‘sources,’ shouldn’t be allowed access to the [Oval Office].” However, in the court filing, Kelly stated that “temporary restraining orders are generally unappealable.” Instead, during the 14-day period, the court “will set a schedule for expedited briefing on a motion for a preliminary injunction,” allowing Trump and the media outlets to submit further evidence for Kelly to consider. Trump has a turbulent history with the media. In 2018, he revoked the White House press credentials of Jim Acosta, who was then a CNN correspondent, after a tense exchange during a news conference. CNN challenged the decision in court and it was Judge Kelly who ordered the White House to reinstate the reporter’s press pass. There has been an outpouring of solidarity among other media outlets in light of the ban. CNN, one of five U.S. news outlets in the White House press pool that rotates responsibility for pooled live video coverage, was scheduled to cover Trump's trip to the U.N. General Assembly in New York on Monday. When they were restricted from doing so, other major television networks declined to replace CNN’s pool crew. In a rare joint statement, ABC, CBS, CNN, Fox News, and NBC said: “The public has a vital interest in receiving accurate, independent information about its government. No Administration should restrict a news organization because it objects to its reporting.” In a further show of support, some media publications—including the New York Times and Washington Post—announced they would hold off on publishing or distributing still photographs taken as part of the White House pool coverage. On Wednesday, 49 media organizations filed a legal brief voicing their support of CNN, MS NOW, and Politico. “Stripping journalists and news outlets of their rights because of perceived editorial viewpoint violates decades of Supreme Court jurisprudence and is impermissible in any type of forum, including the White House,” the brief argued.

Exclusive: The Admiral Who Must Prevent War With China
2026-09-24

Exclusive: The Admiral Who Must Prevent War With China

TIME | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Samuel Paparo aboard the USS Theodore Roosevelt at Pearl Harbor on July 29, 2026. In the summer of 1986, when he was a college student in Pennsylvania, Samuel Paparo took his future wife Maureen to watch Top Gun. Many young Americans emerged from theaters across the country with a new hero: the brash, daring, risk-taking Maverick, played by Tom Cruise, who became an avatar of American power at the twilight of the Cold War. But Paparo took away a different lesson. “The hero of the movie is supposed to be Maverick, but the hero of the movie is actually Iceman,” Paparo tells TIME, referring to the main character’s rival at the elite Navy pilot training school. “Maverick’s got all the personal charisma, but Iceman is fastidious, quiet. He’s the hard-working guy that does the right thing, that doesn’t do flashy, dangerous stuff for its own sake.” Paparo had already passed the Navy’s entrance exams when he went into the theater that day, and joined the next year. His career would precisely mirror the fictional Iceman’s: He attended the Navy Fighter Weapons School, also known as TOPGUN; became a fighter pilot; then a four-star admiral; and eventually commanded the Pacific Fleet. But Paparo would go further, taking the helm of the U.S. Pacific Command, the nation’s oldest and largest geographic combatant command, which covers about half of the earth’s surface. Few Americans will have heard of Admiral Paparo, and that suits him fine. “If I do my job right, history will never know my name,” he says in his office at the headquarters of the U.S. Pacific Command in Hawaii, on a hill overlooking Pearl Harbor, in late July. But his role places him at an inflection point, as American dominance in the Pacific faces its most serious challenge since World War II. His command is designed to be the immovable object that blocks the unstoppable force of China’s expansionist ambitions in the region. And those ambitions have been stated clearly: Beijing has vowed to bring Taiwan, the self-ruling island that has been politically separate from the mainland since China’s civil war ended in 1949, under its control—by force if necessary. China’s President Xi Jinping has described unification as “a historical inevitability.” Paparo’s job, in its simplest terms, is the projection of American power. His most important mission is to deter a Chinese invasion of Taiwan, an event that would challenge the very foundations of a global system underpinned by American hegemony. That the outlines of this mission stay the same regardless of which party or President is in the White House speaks to the importance of his role. The force Paparo builds and the plans he puts in place last for decades, and could determine to whom the next century belongs. The public face of American military power is, of course, the Commander in Chief. The public voice emanates from the Oval Office and the podium in the White House press briefing room. But behind that power sits commanders like Paparo, overseeing the often hidden and awesome infrastructure that backs up that public face and voice—his command oversees some 400,000 military and civilian personnel, a Pacific Fleet of some 200 ships, including five aircraft carrier strike groups, and more than 1,000 aircraft. He marshals that power in distant oceans, islands, and military bases that most Americans will never see or even think about. With President Donald Trump set to meet Thursday with China’s Xi for their third face-to-face summit since he returned to office, the cameras are trained on the White House; reporters will parse each utterance by the two leaders to divine whether the two powers are heading toward cooperation or conflict. It is Paparo’s job to ensure that whatever happens when the leaders talk, U.S. power remains pre-eminent—and that if somehow in the future conflict breaks out, it comes out on top. He must hold to that approach even as wars elsewhere consume the munitions and resources he may need, and upheaval at the Pentagon raises the stakes of pushing back. “I’m almost quoting Sun Tzu when I say winning without fighting is the acme of skill,” he says, referencing the Chinese military strategist who wrote The Art of War. “Mission success for me is not to fight and win a war; it’s to provide a deterrent capability so that we never go to war in the first place.” Paparo, 61, speaks deliberately and in a low voice, smiling often. But when conversation turns serious, so does his expression—his brows become furrowed, and he stares into the middle distance to summon his words. “We don’t seek to become great wartime leaders,” he says. “We seek to be great peacetime deterrers.” What if deterrence doesn’t work? The U.S. emerged from World War II with its dominance of the Pacific unquestioned, but that may now be changing. Over the past few decades, as China has transformed into the world’s second largest economy, it has been engaged in a historic military buildup. China’s defense spending has increased 13-fold over the past three decades, and it now spends more on defense than the next 22 Indo-Pacific countries combined, according to the Center for Strategic and International Studies (CSIS), a bipartisan Washington, D.C.–based think tank. It has made rapid technological advances in artificial intelligence and fielded several generations of fighter jets. Its navy has grown from a coastal force into something capable of projecting global power, and by hull count it is now the largest in the world. Brian Hart, deputy director of the China Power Project at CSIS, describes it as a “comprehensive, across-the-board modernization effort,” one that has changed the calculus for every power in the Pacific. The People’s Liberation Army, he tells TIME, has “significantly transformed the balance of power.” Paparo need only look out his office window at the turquoise waters of Pearl Harbor to be reminded of the last time U.S. supremacy in the Pacific was truly tested. He describes China’s buildup as “historic” and “an exponential increase in military power, and more specifically, in power projection capability.” “Twenty-five years ago the People’s Liberation Army Navy was about 37 warships, and today it’s 12 times that,” he says. “Just in the last 18 months they’ve delivered two cruisers, eight destroyers, eight frigates, an aircraft carrier, 11 submarines, four amphibious ships, and four long-range combat logistics ships.” He is particularly concerned by China’s advances in space and missile development and the rapid expansion of its nuclear arsenal. “They’re moving toward world reach,” he says. For all of its global ambitions, much of China’s military development has been pursued with one target in mind. U.S. intelligence assesses that China’s Xi has ordered his military to be capable of seizing Taiwan by force by 2027, the centenary of the People’s Liberation Army. The intelligence community’s annual threat assessment, released in March, suggests no invasion is currently planned for next year, but by then, the pieces Xi needs to do so will likely be in place. If there appears to be a path to capturing Taiwan without using force, Xi insists he would prefer to take it. Presidential elections in Taiwan in January 2028 may provide some clarity in that regard: China’s old rival, the Kuomintang, is increasingly amenable to negotiations with Beijing. If the political party wins in 2028, the likelihood of an invasion may decrease. If Taiwan’s President Lai Ching-te, a fierce opponent of China’s sovereignty claims, wins reelection, Hart says, “Beijing could significantly ramp up pressure on Taiwan.” Paparo’s job is to convince Xi that the costs of military action will be too much to bear, regardless of what is happening politically on the island. By act of Congress, the U.S. is obliged to sell Taiwan the weapons it needs for its own defense; while it does not require the U.S. to defend the island directly, the commitment could go further to include direct conflict with China. He describes his mission as a delicate balancing act between ensuring that his counterparts know American capabilities well enough to understand the risks of engaging, and protecting its necessary secrets and strategy. “It’s demonstrating credible combat power, demonstrating capability and will, but keeping the crown jewels of the nation that are going to deliver combat success opaque to your counterparts, so that they can’t build a counter for it,” he says. Paparo describes the strategy as deterrence by denial. “It’s a way of expressing that we’re not seeking the overthrow of the Communist Party,” he says. “It’s just that you should know that where America’s direct vital interests are involved, there’s a high probability of denying your aggression.” This theory of deterrence operates on a grand, geopolitical scale, and in much smaller interactions. Paparo recounts a confrontation with a Chinese counterpart over what he describes as an “unsafe” encounter in June 2023 during a transit of the Taiwan Strait in which a Chinese Luyang destroyer cut across the bow of an American ship. In the ensuing back-and-forth, Paparo told his counterpart that he had been in command at the watch center for the engagement and had video showing that their version of events was not true. He offered to share it, then let the matter rest. “I’m not going to impugn his own character in it. He’s representing his country. He’s in uniform, and you know, if we do have a fight, him and I might have to shake hands at the end and agree to go to our separate sides,” he says. In that moment, Paparo chose a diplomatic approach. He does, however, have a more direct plan to deter a Chinese attack on Taiwan. It is based on a term he uses to describe the environment Chinese forces would walk into if they took military action: “Hellscape.” The strategy has been partly inspired by Ukraine’s defense against Russian invasion, and its ability to halt Moscow’s advances by turning the front lines of that conflict into a no-man’s-land patrolled by killer drones. Paparo would aim to saturate the Taiwan Strait with cheap, mass-produced precision drones, along with more conventional high-tech missile power, to deny China the ability to land an amphibious assault, or at least significantly delay it. “We’ve seen that assault warfare, whether that is an amphibious assault, an airborne assault, has become a much more costly human endeavor,” he says. “In the ways that Ukraine is about a 600-mile-by-30-mile hellscape of drone warfare, I seek to put those principles at work to deter an armed invasion.” He continues: “Putting 21st century technological change to work for the United States of America is what that’s about. We need not gain air or maritime superiority of a space; we need only deny its use to aggressors.” Direct American involvement in a conflict over Taiwan is by no means assured. Paparo is tasked with building a force to confront China, but the President will decide how to respond. “The highest ideals of American civil-military relations are such that policy is king,” he says. Who would win that conflict is something that is fiercely debated. A 2023 CSIS war game found that the U.S. and its allies defeated a Chinese amphibious invasion of Taiwan in most scenarios, but the costs to the U.S. were substantial: two aircraft carriers, dozens of ships, and hundreds of aircraft, the heaviest American losses since World War II. Those war games were run before the war in Iran depleted U.S. missile stocks. “I am confident in our ability to prevail,” Paparo says. “What I am working toward is the margin by which we do, and that margin expresses itself in lives, capability, money, and time. I want to drive that bill down to zero.” An enormous map of the Indo-Pacific theater lines the wall of Paparo’s office. His desk overlooks a large room adorned with historical Pacific artifacts and gifts given to the admiral and his predecessors: a five-star flag that belonged to General Douglas MacArthur; a barometer gifted to Admiral Harry Harris Jr., who led the command from 2015 to 2018, by China’s Ministry of National Defense. Closer to his desk is a large computer screen showing the positions of every vessel under his command. As he toggles through the ships on the screen to demonstrate the scale of his fleet, he pauses somewhere near the Indian Ocean to press home a point: His command is not merely preparing for war; it is fighting one. “The Iranian frigate was sunk by us,” he says. He is referring to an incident in March, when the U.S.S. Charlotte submarine torpedoed and sank the Iranian navy frigate IRIS Dena in the Indian Ocean as it was returning to Iran from India, where it had taken part in an international exercise. More than 100 Iranian sailors were killed. Paparo’s forces have also helped enforce a blockade of Iran’s coastline and support logistics for the war. That the U.S. Pacific Command is fighting one war on one side of the world while preparing for another 6,000 miles away in the Taiwan Strait is a measure of its scale. As he stands in front of his map, Paparo sweeps his arm down the Western seaboard, along the coast to Southern California, and across the central Pacific, pointing out an array of U.S. territories and possessions. These places may seem like distant lands to many Americans. To Paparo, they are as much a part of the U.S. as New York or California. To see the Pacific in this way is to understand the meaning behind this projection of power: it’s not just for the protection of an important strategic partner in Taiwan; it is for the protection of the U.S. itself. “There’s no more Pacific a country in the world than the United States of America,” he says. It’s not just about Taiwan and China, either. The North Koreans, he says, “are seeking a nuclear deterrent” and want to present the world with a “fait accompli.” The Russians “have increased their air, missile, and maritime capabilities.” Much of the job is managing friends rather than enemies. The Pentagon has called on Paparo to mediate between allies at odds with one another, and he says there is nothing new in that. Those military-to-military relationships, he adds, “tend to be an anchor that attenuates the ups and downs of policy disagreements among political leaders ... but it always respects the national policy of our civilian leadership.” Few conflicts in the world today do not brush up against the Indo-Pacific theater or impact it in some way. The Iran war and U.S. support for Ukraine have placed severe strain on the resources available to all commands, causing a depletion in the stockpiles for missile defense like the Patriot and THAAD systems, and in long-range ground attack missiles, according to the Pentagon and CSIS estimates. In May, a planned $14 billion arms package for Taiwan was put under review in part to preserve munitions for the Iran war. Experts have warned it may take years to replenish those stocks, a timeline that could affect the calculus of China and Russia. Those shortages are no secret, but it’s a perilous time to be out of step with the White House and the Pentagon. At least 20 top military leaders, including generals and admirals, have been fired or left their positions since Pete Hegseth became Secretary of Defense at the start of the second Trump Administration. The turmoil culminated in the late August resignation of Army Secretary Dan Driscoll, who reportedly left his role after clashing with Hegseth over the firings of senior generals and the Secretary’s alleged stymieing of Army modernization efforts in the midst of a U.S. war against Iran. Hegseth has characterized those departures as part of a campaign to undo what he saw as the promotion of “woke” leadership. In a speech in September 2025 in Quantico, Va., to the military’s generals and admirals, all of whom were required to attend, Hegseth criticized the promotion of leaders for what he described as “the wrong reasons, based on their race, based on gender quotas, based on historic so-called firsts.” Paparo was visible in the front row of that gathering, his expression giving nothing away. He has been careful in his public remarks about the impact the war in Iran has had on the readiness of his command. When questioned in the Senate about how prolonged conflict could impinge on his mission, Paparo conceded only that “there are finite limits to the magazine, and I have all the faith in the world that they’re being employed judiciously.” In private, he appears to have been more candid. Paparo’s name emerged recently in a Washington Post report on a classified assessment, along with other combatant commanders, in which he is said to have objected to the continued use of an aircraft carrier strike group and destroyers for the war in Iran. He was also named in a New York Times report as one of several “private interlocutors” who had been offering “unvarnished” views of the Iran war to Vice President JD Vance. Vance’s team declined to comment on the report. Ward Carroll, a Navy veteran who flew in F-14s and a friend of Paparo’s for many years, says the admiral’s circumspection is part of his character. “He doesn’t want headlines,” he says. “He’s not out there like some other folks have been, saying Epic Fury was a huge mistake. He may say it in private ... but he’s certainly not going to say it to the media or allow it to be leaked.” Paparo excels, Carroll says, by choosing not to “wade into the food fight that is modern-day media and politics at all.” A spokesperson for Pacific Command declined to comment on Paparo’s private conversations about the Iran war. When TIME asks Paparo about the potential impact of Ukraine and Iran, he is again cautious, pivoting to the lessons the U.S. is learning from both: namely the rapid deployment of drones in Ukraine and of AI-enabled operations in Iran, which were used to select and recommend targets for the U.S. military. “The nature of an operation in the Pacific would be to counter an amphibious invasion. Well, we’ve just not used a lot of anti-ship weapons—we still maintain robust inventories of those,” he says. “And so, while I’m not going to whistle past the problem of the expenditure of munitions, it is our job to charge into the learning, and for us to get better.” If there is a permanence to America’s projection of power in the Pacific, it can also be found in Paparo’s own family. His father Samuel Paparo Sr., who died in February, served four years in the Marine Corps before becoming a shipworker at the Philadelphia Naval Shipyard. There he helped refit the aircraft carrier U.S.S. Kitty Hawk and repair the U.S.S. John F. Kennedy. The younger Paparo would later fly from both. “He was really proud of that,” he says of his father. His grandfather was a Seabee, serving in one of the Navy’s construction battalions across the Pacific during World War II, in Bora Bora, Guam, and other islands. He died when Paparo was 13, but he left behind an old wooden scrapbook he had built with pictures from the war and all the places he had been. A young Paparo would pick it up from time to time and leaf through it. “I don’t think he imagined his grandson to be here in command,” he says. His mother washed hair in a South Philadelphia barbershop and later worked a makeup counter in a department store. “Neither my father nor his father finished high school. In fact, nobody in my family before me had ever had a university education,” he says. He would later attend Villanova, the Catholic college also attended by the first American Pontiff, Pope Leo XIV. “We make Popes and admirals,” he says of his alma mater. He joined TOPGUN, whose exacting culture left a lasting impression. “What the school confers is attention to detail, dedication to execution, dedication to getting better, and being introspective and running to your own problems, of seeking the perfect flight that you’ll never get,” he adds. He flew F-14, F-15, and F/A-18 jets, and clocked more than 6,000 flight hours and 1,100 carrier landings. He says he initially aimed to serve as long as his father. “Serving for four years was intentional, and serving for 40 was a blessing,” he adds. Paparo’s time leading the Pacific Command is historic not just for the opponent he faces, but also because it coincides with a rapid and dramatic change in the nature of warfare. On one wall of his office hangs a reprint of a TIME cover from May 18, 1942, depicting Admiral Chester Nimitz, the naval commander who led the U.S. to victory over Japan at sea in the Pacific war. Nimitz took command of the Pacific Fleet in December 1941, also at a time of great technological change. After Pearl Harbor, and under Nimitz’s watch, the battleship would be eclipsed by the aircraft carrier. Amphibious warfare would be revolutionized and directed under a single commander. And then there was the kamikaze—organized suicide attacks by aircraft flown by Japanese pilots—and their foreshadowing of the questions posed by the advancement of unmanned aerial drones in warfare today. “He adapted to change, including the unknown,” Paparo says of Nimitz. “That’s the greatest leadership model I can think of.” Paparo hopes that he never has to fight the war he has spent so long preparing for, that his plans go untested and passed on to the next commander. In the meantime, he will continue with the quiet work. “Mine is not a heroic story,” he says. “Heroism is service that happens at the unforgiving hour. The best service prevents the unforgiving hour.”

America, China and the Evolving New World Order
2026-09-24

America, China and the Evolving New World Order

TIME | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump shakes hands with China's President Xi Jinping at the Great Hall of the People in Beijing on May 14, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings On Thursday, President Xi Jinping of China is meeting President Donald Trump at the White House. The United States and China are in significantly different places and the world increasingly fragmented and multi-polar since he last visited the White House to meet  President Barack Obama in 2015. As the American and Chinese leaders meet, the most consequential question is: who is shaping the international order, and according to whose principles and rules? The answer may be more nuanced than many expected: while the U.S. remains the world’s most powerful country, China is emerging as an architect of that order. Beijing is creating institutions, proposing diplomatic initiatives, establishing technological standards and offering infrastructure to countries across the world. But its influence extends beyond diplomacy. China offers developing countries alternative paths to prosperity, mobilizing its technological champions as sources of power. Rather than replicating the American system of military alliance and security guarantees, Beijing is building networks of trade and alternative financial institutions. China is also promoting a new vocabulary of international affairs—one that is deliberately distinct from the American language of power. China does not have a miraculous, surefire plan to succeed the U.S. as the world’s new hegemon. Nor is there evidence that other countries are prepared to embrace a Chinese-led system entirely. China’s ambition appears more complicated: it seeks greater influence over the ideas and institutions that determine how the international system works. Beijing wants not merely a decisive vote in the game, but a hand in writing its rules. Beijing is pursuing this ambition at a moment when Washington, despite its power, appears less willing to shoulder the traditional burdens of global leadership. Its domestic political divisions, the Trump Administration’s apparent disregard for international law, and its erratic approach to foreign policy and global conflicts have raised questions about the future of American stewardship. Yet China is not inheriting a vacuum. Thanks to its power, Washington remains deeply entrenched in the institutions, alliances, and technologies that underpin its supremacy. China is gaining room to shape the international order, but it is not yet replacing the power that built much of it. Instead, Beijing is creating alternatives that allow other countries to become less dependent on the existing American-led system. Perhaps the most powerful element of China’s vision for a different international order is not its defense of national sovereignty or multipolarity, but its proposition about development. Beijing is asking a pointed question: what if countries need not follow the Western path to modernity? For much of the past eight decades, the prevailing vision of development was associated with a broadly Western model: foreign aid, privatization, and political liberalization. China offers something different, defying the hopes of Western intellectuals and policymakers who believed that Beijing’s entry into the World Trade Organization in 2001 would eventually lead to its political liberalization. China’s own experience suggests that economic success can be built, at least partly, through investments in infrastructure, financed by public finance and state loans. The old Chinese expression about “getting richer by building roads” has also shaped its approach abroad: Beijing has committed itself to financing and promoting across the Global South the type of infrastructure that helped power its own economic miracle. The developing world has been receptive. China’s Global Development Initiative places development at the center of its international engagement. Although the Belt and Road Initiative has been heavily criticized since its inception, it has provided Beijing with a practical platform for financing and building infrastructure overseas. Beijing is making its case not through elegantly written standards of governance but through tangible things: roads, railways, and ports. Over the past decade, the Chinese government and its state-owned enterprises invested approximately $634 billion into building physical infrastructure around the world, along with $419 billion in direct investments outside the financial sector in the developing world. For many governments in Latin America—a region Washington has long regarded as its strategic backyard—access to Chinese development finance and Chinese markets has led to significant economic gains. China’s development strategy creates economic relationships that should endure for decades, especially when its financing carries conditions that serve Beijing’s interests, including privileged access to critical minerals and resources. But the era of immense Chinese overseas spending is becoming harder to sustain as Beijing grapples with its own economic woes. The ultimate test of Beijing’s economic statecraft, then, is whether it can build lasting relationships across the globe that rest on more than financial largesse and accumulated diplomatic capital. China must demonstrate that it understands what developing countries actually need and that those needs will evolve over time. Influence is no longer exerted through explicit military might but built quietly into the infrastructure of everyday economic life. As development becomes increasingly intertwined with technology, a country that adopts Chinese cloud computing may also embed itself in a Chinese-led ecosystem that endures for decades. Artificial intelligence offers the clearest example of technology as a source of geopolitical power. China is rapidly promoting its AI capabilities globally, including open-weight models that can be deployed at a fraction of the cost of their Western competitors. For much of the developing world, the appeal is obvious: those countries can gain access to cutting-edge technology without having to rely exclusively on American tech giants. The technological race is also becoming a competition over the development choices countries make. If most economies build their digital infrastructure and AI systems primarily around American technologies, Washington will retain an enormous structural advantage. As countries invest in those systems, they will become increasingly dependent on them, while American tech giants gain access to vast stores of user data. If more countries come to rely on Chinese technologies and the standards and supply chains associated with them, China will acquire greater influence: less visible than military power, but potentially just as consequential and enduring. This is why China’s enormous industrial capacity has become a central instrument of its effort to shape the global order. Beijing is not merely exporting products: it is helping determine which technologies countries use to modernize themselves, and by extension, the systems their economies will rely on. A central ambiguity lies at the heart of China’s development strategy. Chinese technology can expand a country’s economic opportunities but is bound to increase its dependence on China. That tension is likely to persist longer than many anticipated. Many governments are choosing Chinese technology for its affordability and not because they have been persuaded by Beijing’s governing philosophy. A country may adopt Chinese systems while remaining aligned with the U.S. on security. China therefore does not depend on others becoming “pro-China”. It may be enough for them to become less dependent on the West. This is a vital distinction. Beijing need not convince the world that its model is superior. It only needs to show that alternatives to Western technologies exist. The cumulative effect of those alternatives could gradually alter the balance of the global order. China’s statecraft is a way of redistributing economic and technological power. Unlike the U.S., China’s most significant export may not be a political model but the infrastructure and technology through which other countries seek to build prosperity. In adopting those systems, they become part of a world less dominated by the U.S. and its European allies. Before arriving in Washington, President Xi undertook a flurry of overseas travel, visiting places that American presidents have largely neglected: Bishkek, Cairo, New Delhi. His itinerary illuminated an interesting pattern. Each of his September stops connected China to the expanding international networks Beijing has championed and some Western governments regard with suspicion, notably the Shanghai Cooperation Organization (SCO) and BRICS. Consider BRICS. What began as an informal gathering of emerging economies has become a much larger political forum, with countries from the Middle East, Africa, and elsewhere seeking membership or closer connections. It would be a mistake to consider BRICS as a Beijing-led anti-American bloc. Its members have sharply different interests, and countries such as India have no intention of joining an anti-Western alliance. But the group’s expansion reflects a critical shift: a growing number of countries want access to institutions in which the Group of 7 does not set the terms. The Group of 7 is an exclusive club whose members broadly share political values and standards of governance. China does not demand that other countries abandon institutions led by Washington; instead, it builds alternatives that exist alongside them. That distinction matters. This is why I expect that the emerging world order will not be divided neatly between a Chinese camp and an American one. Most countries will continue to use the dollar, trade with the U.S., and participate in Western institutions while simultaneously joining BRICS, borrowing from Chinese-backed institutions, and engaging with the SCO. For many governments, the fundamental attraction is the freedom to choose among competing sources of finance, technology, and diplomatic support. Beijing’s advocacy of multipolarity is neither merely about building a fairer world, nor is it about replacing one hegemon with another. It is an attempt to widen the range of choices available to other states. By establishing new institutions and initiatives, Beijing is accelerating the movement of power away from the traditional centers of the West. For China, being an architect of the international order does not necessarily mean replacing America, but building a world where the U.S. can no longer design all the rules or define legitimate international behavior by itself. Yet the China dilemma remains. Beijing opposes an international order dominated by a single power, but fragmenting that system would inevitably increase China’s global influence. And the more influence China acquires, the more other countries may begin to question whether Beijing’s vision is truly intended to produce greater equality, or merely a new distribution of power that benefits China most. President Xi’s meeting with President Trump will be scrutinized for the usual signs of rivalry: tariffs, technology restrictions, and strategic tensions. But beneath those headlines lies the great contest over how the international order itself should work. China is not seeking to inherit the world America built. It is trying to change how that world works by giving other countries choices beyond the American-led system. For decades, the U.S. was identified with policing the rules-based international order, while China was portrayed primarily as its challenger. But during President Trump's second term, questions of sovereignty and international law have themselves become more contested, blurring what once seemed a clear distinction between the American and Chinese approaches. Whether China’s ascent produces a fairer international order, a more genuinely multipolar one, or simply a new system in which Chinese power carries greater weight remains uncertain. The answer will depend not only on what Beijing wants, but also on what other countries are willing to accept. For now, China’s ambition may not be to write all the rules, but to ensure that Washington is no longer the only power capable of writing them.
TIME
Philanthropy Must Build the Architecture of Hope
2026-09-24

Philanthropy Must Build the Architecture of Hope

TIME | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Children play at a polling station at a school in Chennai, India, on April 23, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings I grew up in a middle class family in Mumbai, where my parents taught us the importance of giving back. Our success, they insisted, was meaningful only if it helped others rise. As a girl, I spent most Sundays accompanying my father as he distributed food to those in need. As a young teacher at an elementary school, I learned that the greatest gift a teacher offers is not knowledge itself but the confidence and capabilities it awakens in a child. And, as a mother, I experienced most profoundly that there is no greater joy than nurturing another life. Mahatma Gandhi taught us that service to others is among life’s highest forms of fulfilment. His ideas have shaped me and my ideas of philanthropy profoundly: sarvodaya, his vision of the welfare of all, and, antyodaya, his insistence on the uplift of society’s most marginalized. But the highest purpose of philanthropy is to make itself obsolete. To do that, we must turn acts of care and generosity into lasting change by building institutions. Societies shape the future through the institutions they build: universities, hospitals, museums, scientific laboratories, and schools. Every generation has a responsibility to pass on stronger institutions to the next. Our world is awash in plenty. Yet roughly between two to three billion people, especially in the Global South, remain deprived of nutritious food, adequate housing, clean water and sanitation, quality education and healthcare, secure livelihoods, and protection from environmental hazards. The primary responsibility for meeting these needs rests with governments, multilateral institutions, and businesses. But social transformation also requires philanthropic institutions capable of combining excellence with compassion, innovation with integrity, and purpose with performance. Across India, I have encountered incredibly talented and dedicated doctors and nurses; teachers and artists; farmers, athletes, and volunteers. Extraordinary things happen when these talented and dedicated people find opportunity and support. This belief guides my work as chair of India’s Reliance Foundation, on every institution we have built—from excellent schools to a global university still taking shape; from high quality, affordable and accessible healthcare to a cultural center that showcases arts and artists from the world’s great cities and India’s remotest towns and villages; from an initiative that offers dignity and opportunity to millions of India’s skilled craftspeople to an organization dedicated to protecting and caring for animals. The most remarkable people I have met rarely ask for aid. What they seek is a chance: a chance to learn, to create, and to contribute. When society provides such opportunities reliably and at scale, lives are transformed. Institutions make this possible. They do more than solving the problems of our today; they prepare humanity for the possibilities of our tomorrow. But building institutions demands patience. Institutions are gifts to those who inherit them; in time, they might create things their founders never imagined. I often think about the world our children, and their children, will inherit. I hope it will be healthier, kinder, more harmonious, more creative, and more com- passionate. I hope it will be a world where every child has the freedom to dream without limits and the means to pursue those dreams with confidence. The Sanskrit phrase Vasudhaiva Kutumbakam teaches us that the world is one family. I believe that institution-building is one of the purest expressions of this philosophy: it enlarges the circle of care to embrace humanity as a whole. And since all nations, communities, and cultures are branches of a single tree, we share a responsibility to tend it together. Philanthropic institutions should forge bonds of mutual learning, exchange, and collaboration; partnerships help multiply the impact of philanthropy. Ultimately, philanthropy is not measured by the wealth we give away but by the futures we make possible, by the dreams that become reality because someone built something lasting. Time may dim the memory of who builds institutions, but the institutions themselves must shine on.

Project work in education is nothing new; it goes back well over a century. And in real life, you may get a STEM degree, but your real education in engineering comes from real life work. But that doesn't mean classes in calculus and physics don't do anything for you. Or English classes, for that matter. Nobody doubts the value of project work in education, particularly *androgogy* -- the education of adults. But for *pedagogy* you have to establish foundational skills as the student matures intellectually and socially. Entering the high school years, a typical student has had *no* training in critical thinking skills. To develop critical thinking skills in an area, you need both general skills (epistemologicla literacy: research, location of orignial sources, dealing with conflicting evidence) and domain specific training in that area. In other words, *liberal arts* training is foundational in an AI-saturated world. "Liberal Arts" -- from *artes liberales*: literally the skills necessary to be a free person. Trump Found a New Horrifying Way to Disappear ICE Detainees
2026-09-24

Trump Found a New Horrifying Way to Disappear ICE Detainees

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
This is Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Click here to receive it in your inbox each week. President Donald Trump and his deputy chief of staff Stephen Miller have transformed America’s immigration system into an astonishingly cruel deportation machine. This week we learned they have once again taken things up a notch. For the past 10 days, the Department of Homeland Security has stopped offering tracking services for anyone arrested and detained with an outstanding removal order, priming detainees to be quickly removed to third countries without ever having spoken to an attorney or even their families. More than 16,000 people are estimated to have been impacted by this new practice, according to a new report by the Associated Press, and immigration attorneys are sounding the alarm. The Department of Homeland Security has been running an online detainee locator since 2010 where anyone can look up a person being held in immigration detention. This is an especially important tool for immigration attorneys, who use this, often exclusively, to track where their clients are being held in order to call them and meet with them for legal counsel—a constitutional right any person being held by the U.S. government has, regardless of immigration status. On Sept. 15, without any real notice, DHS suddenly began to exclude people who have been arrested and detained by Immigration and Customs Enforcement and who have a removal order against them from their database, making it virtually impossible to find out where they are being held. As of early July, ICE detention facilities held more than 65,000 people, and around 30 percent of them likely had final removal orders, according to the AP. Some people who face removal orders have legal protections against their deportation because they face credible threats of torture or persecution in their countries of origin, but the Trump administration has found a loophole with third-country removals. Now, they’ve found yet another workaround: simply not including these folks on DHS’ detainee locator, which is delaying, if not outright preventing, attorneys being able to contact their clients and initiate legal proceedings to protect them from deportation. Meanwhile, since the start of Trump’s second term, the number of deportation flights has been drastically rising, with Human Rights First estimating departures increased by 59 percent in July 2026 alone, compared to the same time last year. Stephanie Alvarez-Jones is an attorney with the National Immigration Project and has been representing people detained by the Trump administration. I spoke with her to see if any of her clients had gone missing from DHS’ detainee locator, what it might have taken to find them, and what this latest move signals about the Trump administration’s motives when it comes to immigration. This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity. Shirin Ali: What goes through your head when you realize a client you’ve been representing is no longer listed on DHS’ detainee locator? Stephanie Alvarez-Jones: A number of my clients and other folks at the National Immigration Project have had this happen to them. The initial reaction is panic, especially as we’re seeing this trend of rapid transfers and third-country removals. When I could no longer find someone, that was where my mind immediately jumped to. I started to plug in my client’s information and was getting a message that this person is no longer in the system and I immediately jumped to: Oh my gosh, how could all of these people have been transferred or removed so quickly, we were just in touch. Are they about to be deported?How do I go about finding all of these folks? We now know that if someone with a removal order is in the process of either being transferred to a detention facility or if they have already been deported, they no longer appear on the ICE locator. You get a message that says the system has changed. Upon realizing your clients were no longer listed in DHS’ system, what steps did you take to try to find them? And how long did it take? It took different amounts of time for different individuals. For some of them, where there were already pending habeas proceedings, for example, I would reach out to opposing counsel to see if they had any knowledge as to where my client was. In other cases, it was waiting to be able to hear from the client directly; they would eventually call me because we’re in fairly regular contact or I would hear from their family members or friends. For some of them, it was just troubleshooting; did I enter the wrong number? Did I do something to mess up? It’s like running things through a couple of times, trying to look them up on different websites and trying to schedule legal appointments with them. It’s sometimes a combination of trying all of these different roundabout mechanisms to try to confirm that these folks were, in fact, still in detention and where I left them, more or less. The longest amount of time it took me to find my clients was about a day or two. However, the caveat there is those individuals were already detained, so I had a starting point. I knew where they were the last time I spoke to them, they were at X detention center. Or we had already filed a habeas case. It’s a very different story if it’s someone that just got detained, because historically, people would be detained and it might take a couple of hours before they started showing up in the ICE detainee locator. From that point on, you would know they’re in custody and someone would be able to track where they’re going, like if ICE transfers them. Now, if one of my clients ends up getting picked up today, for example, and they have a final removal order against them, I’m totally clueless and in the dark because I had no starting point, because ICE is no longer listing these folks at all in their detainee locator. It is very different from here on out for anyone else that gets detained with an active removal order against them because you’re starting from zero. I think unquestionably this is making it much more challenging for both the lawyers representing individuals in detention and for their families, who also look to the ICE locator to figure out where their family member or loved one is so they can either visit them or send money to them. These are essential, life-sustaining resources for an individual in detention, and not being able to find someone just makes that all that much harder. In some cases, I think there might be alternate ways to find someone; you might be able to ask a government attorney or schedule a legal call with someone and be able to know that they’re there. But they’re certainly not a foolproof play, and what this ultimately ends up doing is it puts the burden on the detained person to be able to call out and say, “Hey, I’m in such-and-such place, I think?” Folks get transferred across the country and repeatedly throughout their time in detention, and they should not have to bear the burden of being able to communicate to the outside world that they’re being detained by the government. Is DHS legally obligated to disclose where they are detaining someone? They are required to not impede access to counsel. The way that the immigration detention system works is that you need to know where your client is detained in order to be able to schedule a legal call with them, at a bare minimum. There are other aspects that flow from where someone is, but at its core, you need to know where a client is detained in order to speak to them, and that is what previously the ICE detainee locator allowed you to do. This is very case-specific because in some cases there might be grounds to be able to say, “Hey, I’m trying to communicate with my client and you’re impeding that, tell me where my client is so I can schedule a legal call with them.” In other cases, there might be a court order that prevents a person from being transferred from a particular detention center or district, and that would be a different ground to say, “Hey, my client isn’t showing up anymore, but you know they’re not supposed to be moved without prior court approval. What’s going on?” That’s another mechanism, but again, it’s very case-specific. I think there’re so many things about the way that DHS is operating right now that are meant to isolate and restrict access to counsel, and this is certainly an access-to-counsel issue, but I can’t comment on legal strategy or what might happen there. What are the risks that people face when they cannot speak to their lawyer? There are so many things that communication between an attorney and a client are necessary for. Just because an individual might have a final order of removal against them, that does not mean there’s no further legal process that they can engage in. For example, just in their immigration proceedings, a client might need to speak with their attorney about filing an appeal or a motion to reopen an immigration proceeding, and that is very much based on where the client is. Talking to the client is necessary to get all the information needed for that paperwork; you can’t do something without talking to your client. There’s also the habeas side of things, and there’s been a lot of reporting about how habeas litigation has totally exploded, especially in this past year, and that’s based on where an individual is detained. If ICE is disappearing folks, then attorneys are left to guess and figure out where their client might be detained in order to file a habeas petition that, in many cases for individuals with final removal orders, are really urgent, time-sensitive, emergency petitions. Trying to prevent someone from being unlawfully deported either to their home country or, what we’ve been seeing a lot recently, to a third country that they have absolutely no connection to and where they haven’t been given the opportunity to express any level of fear toward. Reporting this week has made it a lot clearer what these third-country removals actually look like, especially the ones to Africa. It’s a disturbing, horrifying image. The U.S. government has been disappearing people to third countries since almost the start of this administration. Once they’re sent off, they’re often cut off from their communities and from their lawyers. But now the disappearance starts much earlier, from the moment someone is detained here in the United States, and that significantly compounds the harm. Why do you think DHS and ICE are suddenly deciding that they’re going to remove detainees from their locator? I can’t speculate as to their motivations, but everything that ICE has done throughout this administration is meant to make detention harder and to isolate individuals in detention. This is another tool to make it harder for individuals to be in touch with their family and their legal team. It makes it harder for their attorneys to then file any type of relief and protection for that individual. It is absolutely meant to stymie the defense of these individuals in immigration attention. This latest change to ICE’s detainee locator is another prong of this administration’s immigration agenda to close in on our immigrant communities. They all work together, the stripping away of status, the expansion of detention center networks across the country, the removal of funding from immigration organizations—all of these are happening very much not in a vacuum. They are all going toward making it harder to get or maintain status in the United States, and if you are detained, making it harder to survive in detention. The administration has made detention conditions so awful that the cruelty is the point. And then making it harder to even fight when you are fighting to stay in the United States, or to at least avoid deportation to a country where you might face further incarceration. These are all interrelated steps that play into this agenda of cruelty and to push out, regardless of the harm, process, and legality, as many people as possible. We hope you learned a thing or two from this edition of Executive Dysfunction, and if you enjoyed reading it, please consider supporting our legal journalism by becoming a Slate Plus member! She’s joined in conversation by Richard Hasen of UCLA School of Law, and they discuss the latest legal challenge and how it seems to be impacting American voters’ sense of election security with a consequential midterm election just weeks away. The Amicus bonus episode dives into the uncertain future of the shuttered Kennedy Center, with Dahlia Lithwick joined by Madiba K. Dennie of Balls and Strikes to talk about how the president was photographed with a poster reading “Kennedy Center DEMOLISHED.” The storied institution not only represents Trump’s latest obsession with destroying buildings, but America’s troubled history of white supremacy. When Immigration Agents Killed People in January, the Nation Took Notice. What’s Different Now?
2026-09-23

When Immigration Agents Killed People in January, the Nation Took Notice. What’s Different Now?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. The year began with a news story that riveted the country, at least as much as any story can in our attention-fragmented era. The fatal shootings of Renee Good and Alex Pretti by federal immigration agents in Minneapolis sparked nationwide protests and outrage on Capitol Hill, and contributed to the decline in Donald Trump’s approval ratings. But the reaction to two additional spasms of violence involving Immigration and Customs Enforcement this week—a traffic stop that led to a deadly crash in Michigan and a shooting in Texas—has been much more muted, at least so far. The incidents raise the troubling possibility that the country is growing numb to injury and death at the hands of federal agents, even as the toll continues to mount. Many details are still missing, but here’s what we know. On Sunday morning, ICE agents reportedly tried to pull over a driver in Grand Rapids named Mario David Coronado Juarez (his surname has been spelled “Suarez” in some media reports). According to police, Juarez, who is from Guatemala and has worked in construction and lived in Michigan for years, fled in his car before crashing into a tree. Juarez’s car caught fire and he was pronounced dead at the scene. A few hours after Juarez’s death, Wilber Rafael Garces Perez was driving around Austin for DoorDash. A 28-year-old from Venezuela, Garces Perez says he entered the U.S. legally using a process the Biden administration created, applied for asylum, and got a work permit. But a change of address meant he missed a court summons, triggering a deportation order that ICE agents noticed. Garces Perez says the agents twice sideswiped his Toyota Corolla, causing damage, before their unmarked SUVs turned on their lights and sirens. After pulling Garces Perez over, an agent told him he was under arrest. At this point, some details are disputed. Two sources told the New York Times that Garces Perez fled in his car, prompting a short chase. According to the Texas Tribune, Garces Perez asked if he could park somewhere sheltered from traffic. Video and audio analyzed by the Times captured an agent standing beside Garces Perez’s car and firing through the rear passenger window as it rolled forward. One bullet struck Garces Perez in the back. He remained in his car for several minutes before police showed up, ICE handcuffed him, and an ambulance arrived. Garces Perez says doctors couldn’t remove the bullet because it had lodged near his spine and ICE wanted to move him to a detention center, where he remains in custody. He allegedly slept on the cement floor of a cell and told reporters that he was “not doing well.” Yes. In July, ICE agents fatally shot two other men: Lorenzo Salgado Araujo, a 52-year-old father who was driving to work in Houston, and Johan Sebastián Durán Guerrero, a 25-year-old from Colombia, in Biddeford, Maine. The outcry that followed prompted the administration to announce that ICE would stop conducting almost all traffic stops nationwide. But given what happened to Juarez and Garces Perez this week, what became of that policy? Trump rescinded it almost immediately. He praised immigration agents for “doing a GREAT job” and called traffic stops “one of ICE’s most important and effective Crime Fighting tools.” What’s the bigger context of these incidents? Deaths tied to immigration enforcement have spiked. ICE agents and cars have turned out to be a particularly deadly combination: Five of the six fatal ICE shootings since Trump retook office involved traffic stops. The data we have is incomplete, but ICE may also be getting more trigger-happy. According to the Trace, a nonprofit newsroom focused on gun violence, armed agents have killed or injured 24 people since last year, compared to 47 between 2015 and 2021. The death rate in ICE detention facilities has also increased, with some detainees alleging inadequate medical care, severe overcrowding, and abusive treatment by guards. (The Trump administration denies those claims.) Poor training appears to be the other major factor. As the Trump administration rushed to ramp up deportations, ICE slashed its training requirements and launched a recruiting blitz that sometimes resulted in sloppy vetting. The agent who shot Garces Perez joined ICE last year. So did the one who killed Durán Guerrero in Maine, and he also reportedly has a history of psychological issues and spousal abuse. The Washington Post reports that ICE has begun firing some recent recruits, citing “red flags that should have been identified earlier, or poor performance.” Will ICE agents face accountability in these cases? That remains to be seen. DHS says it’s investigating the Garces Perez shooting, but the department has also disputed his version of events, including alleging that he nearly ran over an agent (this may sound familiar). The agent who shot Garces Perez had been instructed to wear a body camera but wasn’t, which could make even an impartial investigation harder. In several previous cases, including Good’s and Pretti’s, administration officials initially gave false accounts of what had happened. No agents have been charged in connection with either shooting, and Good’s and Pretti’s family members are still waiting for accountability. (In the separate case of a shooting that wounded another Minneapolis resident, one ICE agent faces federal charges for allegedly lying to the FBI about what happened. He has pleaded not guilty.) Meanwhile, Michigan Gov. Gretchen Whitmer, a Democrat, has ordered state police to investigate the crash that killed Juarez on Sunday. And if Democrats retake Congress this fall, they could also launch investigations. Similar things keep happening. Police in Evanston, Illinois, are reportedly investigating after ICE agents detained and injured a U.S. citizen on Sunday in an apparent case of mistaken identity. But in the meantime, such incidents risk becoming workaday stories that no longer inspire widespread outrage. On Monday, an immigrant rights group organized a protest in front of the Texas Capitol demanding Garces Perez’s release and accountability for the agent who shot him. About 250 people showed up, a turnout that at least one attendee called disappointing. “I think we haven’t shown the kind of resistance that we should be showing,” one of them told the Times. That was a tough news day, but falling into an anxiety spiral or a doomscroll doesn’t help anything. My colleagues have curated some great stories to help you get refreshed and reenergized this evening. A journey into the heart of Slate’s comments section: When Luke Winkie joined Slate as a staff writer in 2023, he was confronted with the site’s die-hard commenters: a group of “verbose, opinionated, more than a little cranky” readers who aren’t afraid to speak their minds. In honor of Slate’s 30th anniversary, Luke waded into the comments section and discovered tales of love, loss, and a vanishing form of online community. An ode to imperfect fruit: Tangerines that are a breeze to peel. Tomatoes that are perfectly tangy. Sugary cantaloupes that don’t rot. Is this the future of fruit? Chason Gordon investigates the rise of produce bred for perfection, and argues that there are unintended consequences to making fruit too delicious. A gritty throwback to print journalism: Danny Boyle’s new film, Ink, depicts Rupert Murdoch’s takeover of the Sun, turning it into the United Kingdom’s top tabloid paper. Sam Adams argues that while newspapers in real life are dying, this onscreen depiction thrums with life. A dose of feminist wisdom: Weeks after Gloria Steinem’s death, her memoir has been posthumously published. Scaachi Koul writes that the book is a road map for “younger generations to keep up the good fight, to notice the wins where they happen, and to not get discouraged.” And with that, it’s time for me to take my leave. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Republican Judges Defy Supreme Court to Try to Boost GOP in Midterms
2026-09-23

Republican Judges Defy Supreme Court to Try to Boost GOP in Midterms

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. The Republican judges on the 8th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals won’t stop trying to help the GOP hold on to Congress, even if it means defying the Supreme Court. On Tuesday, the 8th Circuit again ruled for Missouri Republicans who want to use new, gerrymandered congressional districts in the November election. The decision sought to overturn an order issued by the U.S. Supreme Court. The 8th Circuit was already smacked down by the Supreme Court—twice—for intervening in this lawsuit, which is entirely about state law and not federal law. But the three-judge panel, which includes a couple of Donald Trump appointees and another Republican, ordered Missouri to use the districts that violate state law for a third time. The ruling was swiftly appealed, and Justice Brett Kavanaugh ordered the lawmakers seeking to continue with the GOP’s preferred map to respond by Wednesday afternoon. In the meantime, the panel has ordered the state to use the new districts for the general election, because the Republican secretary of state used them for the primary. The judges noted that if the districts that comply with state law are used, “more than 1.4 million Missourians could not vote for the congressional district nominees who will appear on their November 2026 general election ballot.” The panel cited the elections clause of the U.S. Constitution when it created a new right to vote in the same district in both the primary and general elections. But that clause gives states the power only to regulate the “time, place, and manner” of elections. The ruling radically expands the power of federal courts and state legislatures under the elections clause, something the Supreme Court has repeatedly declined to do. And law professor Justin Levitt notes that there are many other circumstances in which general elections include candidates who weren’t on the primary ballot, such as when a candidate withdraws or a voter moves to a new district after a primary. “The 8th Circuit didn’t bother to explain whether any of those circumstances also violates a constitutional provision that doesn’t ever mention primary elections,” Levitt notes. It’s worth adding that primary elections are an entirely modern construction. Most notably, the ruling conflicts with the binding order from the Supreme Court. The voters who challenged the new districts immediately asked the 8th Circuit to undo its decision, warning that the panel had “revived in substance the very relief the Supreme Court stayed—in direct conflict with the Court’s order.” Some have blamed the Supreme Court justices for setting a bad example for other federal judges by regularly disregarding precedent and any sense of judicial restraint. The panel shifted the blame for the chaos to the state Supreme Court and its “last minute” ruling, while failing to fault the secretary of state for his needless eight-month delay before he rejected a petition that would have invalidated the new map in 2026. The judges smugly said they “trust that the Missouri Supreme Court will respect this court’s decision,” because federal law is supreme throughout the country. But the 8th Circuit seems to have forgotten that the Missouri Supreme Court gets the final say on state law. And if these congressional districts don’t comply with state law, they can’t go into effect. The federal judges claimed they weren’t overturning the Missouri court’s ruling on the state-law issue. But they also suggested that federal courts must intervene in a state-law matter if it influences federal elections. “Allowing state courts to issue, without any federal-court review, last-minute decisions affecting the conduct of federal elections would … potentially allow a handful of state judges to render decisions that decide the outcome of a presidential election or party control of Congress,” they noted. The U.S. Constitution, though, allows states to establish the rules, and federal courts can’t intervene, unless there’s a federal law at issue, such as the Voting Rights Act or the U.S. Constitution. Earlier this month, the Missouri Supreme Court ruled in favor of 300,000 voters who signed a petition demanding a referendum on the gerrymandered districts, which Trump had requested last year. The secretary of state waited until the day of the primary election to reject the petition, but the state Supreme Court overturned his decision and ordered him to use the districts enacted in 2022, even though he had held the primary in the new districts. Instead of complying, the secretary ran to a Trump-appointed federal judge and asked him to order the state to use the new districts. The Missouri Supreme Court—which includes a majority of Republican appointees—wasn’t happy and threatened to hold him in contempt for not immediately complying, which led lawmakers to threaten impeachment if they did. The 8th Circuit upheld the federal judge’s decision, but the U.S. Supreme Court overruled him and deferred to the state court. Federal courts, including the U.S. Supreme Court, have no power to overturn the Missouri Supreme Court’s interpretation of state law. The justices in Washington have declined several Republican attempts to upset state-court rulings on gerrymandering. In 2022, they rejected an appeal from North Carolina Republicans seeking to undo a state court ruling that required fairer election districts. Like the 8th Circuit, lawmakers in that case had argued that the state-court ruling violated the elections clause. Since then, the justices have rejected GOP appeals from other states that relied on similar arguments. Despite all this, the 8th Circuit just won’t stop trying to insert itself into this gerrymandering fight to help the GOP. Even the U.S. Supreme Court, which now gives Republicans virtually everything they want in redistricting cases, rejected this appeal. Twice! And nothing has changed with respect to the federal law claims. That’s why the justices are expected to overrule the 8th Circuit for a third time. Voters in Ohio were not so lucky a few years ago. Judge Amal Thapar, a Trump appointee, ordered the state to use election districts that had been repeatedly ruled unconstitutional by a bipartisan majority on the Ohio Supreme Court. Thapar’s ruling was never overturned, and voters cast their ballots in gerrymandered districts. If the U.S. Supreme Court does the right thing again, Missouri will have to scramble to hold the election in different districts than it used for the primary. Voters in the state have also filed multimillion-dollar lawsuits against the secretary for failing to approve the petition. The 8th Circuit’s actions show that some federal judges will even try to defy the U.S. Supreme Court to help Republicans maintain the unfair advantage that they get through gerrymandering.
