News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Trump’s Threats Against the 2026 Election Are Far From Over
2026-09-23

Trump’s Threats Against the 2026 Election Are Far From Over

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. The Trump administration’s efforts to demobilize and delegitimize the upcoming midterms hardly began or ended with the Hail Mary efforts to take over voting by mail. Indeed, even as the Supreme Court seems to have halted that attempt in its tracks, there are hydralike tentacles of election subversion that have risen up in its stead. On this week’s Amicus podcast, Dahlia Lithwick spoke with one of her ride-or-die elections experts, professor Richard Hasen of the University of California, Los Angeles School of Law, about what’s coming next and how to calibrate panic vs. preparedness dials in the upcoming weeks as we look forward to more election threats while still trying to get this democracy thing right. Hasen is the founding director of the Safeguarding Democracy Project and the author of Election Meltdown: Dirty Tricks, Distrust, and the Threat to American Democracy. A portion of their conversation, which has been edited and condensed for clarity, appears below. Dahlia Lithwick: Last week the Supreme Court finally pulled the plug on Donald Trump’s mail- voting shenanigans. There was some real fear that this would go the other way, but it seems to be a fairly conclusive, very brief order by the court that essentially says the application for the stay that was presented to the court is denied, right? Should we worry that two of the nine justices legitimately thought not only that the administration should have won, but that this was an emergency that should have forced, as you wrote, every state to not be able to do mail voting? Rick Hasen: Well, the first thing to say is that even though the unsigned majority opinion was only three sentences, those sentences packed a punch. I filed a brief with three other law professors, and we focused on what’s called the balance of the equities, which is this idea that when you’re talking about a preliminary injunction or a stay, something that’s going to have to be done in the interim until the court can take a full look at the case, not only do you look at which side’s likely to win on the merits (i.e., does the post office have the authority to do this?); you also look at: What’s the harm going to be to the other side, and what’s the public interest here? The brief that we wrote basically said that you don’t even need to look at whether the U.S. Postal Service has the authority to do this. On the one hand, the government said that there was a problem with voter fraud but did nothing in the district court to prove it, and the district court made a finding that this wouldn’t help stop any voter fraud. On the other hand, you have literally millions of people that would be disenfranchised and election administrators that had run out of time. Ballots have already gone out in some states. There’s just no time. This is so lopsided. What’s notable is, not only did the court say that the equitable factors here didn’t support a stay, they also said the government was likely to lose on the merits. That was unnecessary to reach their decision. That indicates that there are at least five, and up to six, justices who believe that on the merits, the post office is going to lose. That’s really good news. Samuel Alito did not even mention the disenfranchisement risk—and it can’t be that he didn’t know about it; it was our whole brief, and the state of California’s brief did it, and the League of Women Voters’ brief did it. The government itself in its brief didn’t address this at all. Alito just erases the voters. The voters are just not in his calculation of the balance of the equities. That is terrible. But we can take some solace. We can celebrate the good news that there are only two justices who are willing to sign on to this. And even Justice Brett Kavanaugh, who said he kind of agreed with the merits about the USPS’s power to do this, said that on these facts, at this time, this stay cannot be put into place. The president has never been one to take his losses sitting down. Are we going to get an end run around the court? Are we going to see another attempt to thwart the ability to vote by mail in November? I’m much more worried about other things. Let me say this: I think voting by mail is going to be protected in this election, and we’ll have to see what happens in elections going forward. But if you are a person who can easily vote early in person rather than by mail, I’d suggest doing that just because, putting aside all the Trump stuff, you’re more likely if you vote by mail to not have your ballot counted because you forgot to include your signature or you put the wrong date on the ballot. Or in some states you need to have a witness, or you might have filled it out wrong. If you were in a polling place, voting in person, if your ballot is rejected, you can fix it. So, all else being equal, voting early in person is a little bit of a safer way to vote. That has nothing to do with the Trump administration, but it does have to do generally with how people vote, and in some states you’re not told if your ballot doesn’t count, so you would never know. I’m mindful of the fact that there is such a fine line between what we’re calling catastrophizing and what is explaining possible scenarios and really trying to see around corners and do a little disaster planning. I can’t tell you how many conversations I’ve had where someone says, “Even mentioning this can stir up fear and doubt.” I’d like you to take us out of this conversation on the same note you just sounded about mail-in voting, which is that there are things that not just judges, not just poll watchers, and not just election officials but ordinary people can and should do, and you don’t have to think much further back than the people of Hungary who went out to the polls, despite lots of threats, and removed an authoritarian who had access to the levers of government. What are you telling ordinary people who are careening back and forth with the news cycles and really deeply afraid in ways that only serve to cast doubt on whether elections can ever be conducted fairly? Take me on to the side of the line that is information and not catastrophizing, and talk about what it is that people who are worried can and should be doing right now. I should say, first of all, that I don’t think we should just not talk about it. I think that the public discussion about the USPS rule and the mass disenfranchisement it would have caused had the Supreme Court ruled the other way brings attention to this issue for the court. It put pressure on the court so they can’t just do it, the way they did a year ago last summer, when they all of a sudden put the Voting Rights Act up for grabs in an order they issued late on a Friday in August. We have to pay attention, and we have to make sure the public’s paying attention. That said, I think there’s a whole industry out there of what my colleague Justin Levitt calls election law porn. And he’s talking about all of these stories: Mike Johnson is going to seize power! He’s going to not seat Democrats! Or It doesn’t matter what you say because Trump is going to call out the military and declare a national emergency! You hear a lot of this stuff, and the fact is, Trump doesn’t have these powers. He’s not omnipotent. He’s no election genius. In fact, I think if this Postal Service rule had come in, it would actually have been the Democratic cities that would have been much better at getting voters to vote in person than the rural areas. It would have been rural voters most hurt by a rule that shut down mail balloting. So how do you walk that fine line? I think it’s about resilience, right? Not catastrophizing but resilience. Let’s get past this Trump era. That’s what we need to do. We need to put our heads down, hold two more fair elections, and maybe things will be better after that. That means we’ve got to pay attention to all the shenanigans. This is what I do for a living, and I can’t even keep track of all the litigation, but there are lots of great lawyers out there doing the work in the trenches; fighting the Department of Homeland Security rule that’s trying to create an unreliable list of citizens being used to disenfranchise voters. Support those lawyers who are doing this work—there are some great public interest law firms doing this kind of work. And support your local election administrator. Be ready to volunteer as a poll worker. Be an election observer. If you’re not worried about your state, maybe go to a neighboring state. There are lots of opportunities to be involved. First and foremost, make sure you’re registered and that you vote. That’s the way to stick it to people who want to disenfranchise you: to actually vote. And so many people might not think that it matters, but it does matter. One person’s vote is probably not going to make a difference, but if we all had that attitude, we wouldn’t have a democracy. People need to get off their couches and go and vote. And if it’s voting by mail, you might even be able to do it from your couch. But cast that ballot, and do it as soon as you’re ready to make a decision. As I was trying to convince people we were on the ledge in 2020 when we were talking, back during our election meltdown series, I’m now trying to talk some people off the ledge. I think we should not give Trump the power that he does not have. I think we should recognize that we have three bulwarks of protection against attempted election subversion. No. 1, we have the courts, and the courts haven’t done a great job on voting rights, but they’ve done a pretty good job stopping election subversion, in stopping the actual stealing of elections. And the USPS order is the most recent good example of that. Second, we’ve got the states. We have a decentralized election system that causes a lot of problems. I’ve pointed them out in the past. But when it comes to an authoritarian trying to assert control, decentralized power helps. And the third is us, the people. We’re the bulwark. If Trump is dumb enough to send ICE agents to the polls, we need to be out in the streets protecting voters and making sure they can cast their ballots. We’re going to get through this. Someone went shopping in ASUS's eShop – for customer data
2026-09-24

Someone went shopping in ASUS's eShop – for customer data

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Contact details and order records accessed, but PC maker is keeping schtum on how many customers are affected Asus has warned eShop customers that an intruder got into part of its online store and may have helped themselves to contact details and order records. The PC maker disclosed the incident in an email sent to customers, first reported by KitGuru, in which it said had identified "unauthorized access to part of the Asus eShop environment," although exactly when that access occurred remains unclear. "Our investigation indicates that certain customer order information, including contact details and order records, may have been accessed," the company said. There is at least some good news for anyone who has handed Asus their card details. The company said no payment card, bank account, or other financial information was involved in the breach. Asus also said it isn't currently aware of the compromised information being misused or of any affected customers suffering harm. The company said it took steps to contain the incident after discovering the unauthorized access, launched an investigation, and introduced additional measures to secure the affected systems. That investigation remains ongoing, but Asus said it had found no evidence of continued unauthorized access. What Asus hasn't said is how many customers are caught up in the mess, when the intrusion began, how long the attacker had access, which countries are affected, or how whoever was behind the break-in managed to get into the eShop environment in the first place. The details that did escape, however, could give scammers a decent head start. Asus warned that the stolen details could give scammers enough to make phishing emails, texts, and phone calls about its products or customers' orders look rather more convincing. Asus told customers to keep an eye out for unexpected messages mentioning previous purchases, though it reckons the risk of anyone actually misusing the data remains low. This isn't the PC maker's first recent brush with data thieves. In December, Asus confirmed that one of its suppliers had been hacked after the Everest ransomware gang claimed to have pinched 1 TB of data from itself, ArcSoft, and Qualcomm. The company said the haul included some camera source code used in its phones, but maintained that its own systems and customer data were untouched. The Register asked Asus for more details about the latest breach, including how many customers were affected and when and how the intrusion occurred, but has not yet received a response. Asus is yet to comment publicly on the incident, and there is no mention of the breach on its eShop. So it's the usual post-breach drill: beware unexpected emails, texts, and calls. Except this time, whoever's behind them may have the receipts. ® Contact details and order records accessed, but PC maker is keeping schtum on how many customers are affected Matt Rosoff chairs a Register roundtable with HPE and NVIDIA on data sovereignty in the AI era, off the record, in Washington PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity A desktop pitch and an attempt to curb LLM slop descended into very human mayhem Kids can learn why BGP matters in a semester, but that won’t leave them ready to implement it Russian devs alleged to have built tools for US agencies as software from same op was sold to Moscow's security services Gemini 3.8 Flash Cyber and Wiz's Red Agent team up to protect hospitals, public transit, and tech Developers test what they can build with TypeSafe's fast, typed decision model Anthropic and OpenAI debut Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Sol and Luna 'first' publicly documented Windows implant to use LLMs for C2 China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' Ask HPE and NVIDIA the AI data question you cannot ask in public
2026-09-24

Ask HPE and NVIDIA the AI data question you cannot ask in public

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Matt Rosoff chairs a Register roundtable with HPE and NVIDIA on data sovereignty in the AI era, off the record, in Washington EVENT: HPE and NVIDIA are sending their AI infrastructure leads to a dinner table in Washington where nothing said is on the record. The Register is hosting, our editor-in-chief Matt Rosoff takes the chair, and there is one table, on Thursday 29 October. Most AI programs in regulated industries do not stall on the model. They stall on where the data lives, who can reach it, and what an auditor will accept as evidence a year from now. Sovereignty is not one requirement either: what can leave a jurisdiction and what can leave the building are different questions with different owners. The proprietary data that makes an AI system worth building is the same data that makes it a risk to build, and the boundary drawn around a pilot quietly becomes the boundary around production. Those calls get made on what people will tell you in private. What a bank had to rebuild. What a hospital group's legal team refused to sign. Which sovereign option somebody paid for and would not buy again. None of that gets said on a recorded webinar, or in a briefing with a deck. So this is neither. Chatham House Rule, three questions, dinner, no slides. Everyone at the table is there as a peer, the vendors included, and the chair's job includes redirecting anyone who starts to pitch. On the table: which infrastructure options actually hold a boundary, what each one costs in capital, in capability or in elasticity, and whether any of it survives the move from first planning to running operations. HPE sends Thierry Pienaar, HPE Fellow and Chief Technology Officer for HPC and AI Worldwide Sales. NVIDIA sends Bert Condensa, Vice President for Enterprise AI Factory Segment Sales. They are there to answer, not to present. The table is for technology, data and risk leaders in healthcare, financial services, research, pharmaceuticals, government and higher education who are making these decisions now rather than next year. Dinner runs from 6.00pm to 8.30pm at Fiola Mare. Places are limited and go by invitation. Government and public sector guests: where your agency's ethics rules require it, you can pay your own share and we will provide a receipt. Please say so when you request an invitation. Talk of AI in KDE sets the community ablaze
2026-09-24

Talk of AI in KDE sets the community ablaze

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
A desktop pitch and an attempt to curb LLM slop descended into very human mayhem A proposal to make KDE an "AI-native" desktop has gone down like a house on fire: screams, flames, people running for safety. There may be no survivors.* Last weekend was KDE's annual Akademy conference and it included a presentation proposing an AI-native KDE. We noted that it seemed likely to polarize the audience. It looks like this was a considerable understatement. In the days since, a debate over proposed restrictions on LLM-assisted contributions descended into bans and a deleted thread. An outside campaign called for KDE to prohibit AI altogether, a GNOME developer proposed a similar policy for that project, and KDE developer Nate Graham apologized for his role in the uproar. The Akademy talk was titled "A lovable, sovereign, AI-native KDE" and the slide deck [PDF] is now available. It ends by saying: "The question is not whether AI. It is how. You choose how much AI – and which." It seems almost calculated to provoke an argument rather than invite discussion. We do not know if the authors intended this – we attempted to contact both of them, but they have not yet responded. Graham opened a discussion on Invent, KDE's GitLab instance, about proposed restrictions on LLM-assisted contributions. Although we are not a member, we watched this with some interest over the weekend. It rapidly became heated. Moderators issued warnings, restricted further comments, and eventually removed the thread. There is an archive of the discussion, but we warn you, it contains some highly offensive language, albeit censored by the member who quoted another member's tweets on X. As events unfolded, the participant who drew attention to the posts was banned first. The author of the offensive material was banned later after their identity was verified. The discussion is gone, but the argument continues. One response was an outside initiative called KDE for People, which called on the KDE project to adopt a No-AI policy. Around 250 people signed it before its organizers closed it to further signatures. GNOME developer Jordan Petridis has also published The GNOME LLM Policy That I Want, proposing that LLMs be barred from creating or modifying anything submitted to GNOME or hosted on its infrastructure. This mentions the recent ballot on AI usage in Debian. We reported on the developer referendum about a month ago, noting that the broad spread of anti-AI options in the ballot was likely to split the vote. (This likelihood was dismissed in the comments.) Well, as The Register's Asia-Pacific desk reported a few days later, Debian did not ban AI contributions. Graham subsequently explained his involvement in a post titled KDE and AI, and you, and me. He opens by saying: "So I accidentally triggered an online shitstorm in the process of trying to craft a set of more restrictive LLM usage guidelines for KDE. Sorry about that." He notes that the Akademy talk met "what I'm told was a fairly chilly reception" and that "the next day, a workshop was held about the topic, also receiving a chilly reception." Every couple of years, the KDE project sets three goals for the next two years. Earlier this week, after Akademy, it chose its latest three: you can see them in the last column of the goal-setting Kanban board. The ones chosen are: KDE for Enterprise and Deployments (Issue #5) Next Generation Styling for KDE (Issue #3) We see no mention of AI in there. DoJ: Uncle Sam bought forensics software from same Russian operation supplying FSB
2026-09-24

DoJ: Uncle Sam bought forensics software from same Russian operation supplying FSB

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Russian devs alleged to have built tools for US agencies as software from same op was sold to Moscow's security services US defense and homeland security agencies bought digital forensics software developed by the same Russian operation that also sold its wares to the FSB, according to US prosecutors. The Department of Justice says Oxygen Forensics CEO Lee Reiber and Russian national Oleg Sergeyevich Davydov have been arrested and charged with conspiracy to commit wire fraud over an alleged scheme to hide the company's Russian ownership and where its software was actually being built. Oxygen Forensics, based in Virginia, sold digital forensics tools to the US Department of War and several Department of Homeland Security components, including Homeland Security Investigations, the DHS Office of Inspector General, and the Secret Service's National Computer Forensics Institute (NCFI). Prosecutors allege Oxygen presented itself to Uncle Sam as an independent US company, when it was actually owned and controlled by five Russian nationals through a Cyprus-based holding company. Those same five people also owned a Russian outfit formerly known as Oxygen Software LLC and now called MKO Systems LLC. According to the DoJ, the developers who wrote the software worked in Russia, while MKO sold the software there under different product names to customers reportedly including the FSB, the Russian Investigative Committee, and the Russian Ministry of Internal Affairs. In other words, the prosecutors are claiming that while US agencies bought Oxygen's forensic tools, software from the same Russian development operation was being supplied to some very different government customers back home. The alleged concealment kicked up a gear after Russia invaded Ukraine in 2022 and Washington expanded sanctions. Prosecutors claim Reiber was installed as Oxygen's CEO, president, and chairman, while its Russian owners disappeared from public corporate filings but continued making significant decisions, such as setting Reiber's compensation, overruling him on payments, and retaining signatory authority over company bank accounts. When a reporter came knocking about Oxygen's Russian links in 2023, Reiber allegedly told the owners the resulting coverage "could destroy this entire opportunity" – a reference to a contract the company was chasing with NCFI. Oxygen's "current existence ... hangs in the balance," he is said to have added. NCFI went on to award Oxygen a five-year software contract in September 2024. According to prosecutors, Oxygen had separately told the Department of Defense that no foreign person had the power to control the company or its decisions. As recently as March 2026, Reiber allegedly told DHS personnel that no Russian was involved in developing the software and nobody in Russia had access to its build environment. The DoJ claims the software was in fact written and managed by a Russian team under Davydov's direction. Reiber was arrested in Idaho, while Davydov was nabbed at London's Heathrow Airport before boarding a flight to Istanbul. The US plans to seek his extradition. Authorities have also seized corporate bank accounts, around 57 domains, and other infrastructure. The DOJ is not alleging that Oxygen's software contained malicious code or was used to gain unauthorized access to customers' systems or data. Both men, if convicted, could be swapping forensic software for prison bars for up to 20 years. ® Contact details and order records accessed, but PC maker is keeping schtum on how many customers are affected Matt Rosoff chairs a Register roundtable with HPE and NVIDIA on data sovereignty in the AI era, off the record, in Washington PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity A desktop pitch and an attempt to curb LLM slop descended into very human mayhem Kids can learn why BGP matters in a semester, but that won’t leave them ready to implement it Russian devs alleged to have built tools for US agencies as software from same op was sold to Moscow's security services Gemini 3.8 Flash Cyber and Wiz's Red Agent team up to protect hospitals, public transit, and tech Developers test what they can build with TypeSafe's fast, typed decision model Anthropic and OpenAI debut Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Sol and Luna 'first' publicly documented Windows implant to use LLMs for C2 China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' Google to critical infra orgs: Our AI scanners won't be evil, promise
2026-09-24

Google to critical infra orgs: Our AI scanners won't be evil, promise

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Gemini 3.8 Flash Cyber and Wiz's Red Agent team up to protect hospitals, public transit, and tech Google has jumped on the AI-defense-for-critical-systems train with its Scan for Good initiative, and says that its threat-hunting models have already autonomously uncovered critical security issues at hospitals, a municipality, a public rail operator, and major technology providers. So don't fear these bots. The initiative, announced on Thursday, uses Google’s Gemini 3.8 Flash Cyber, a version of the model tuned for software bug hunting and remediation, and Wiz’s Red Agent - this is the Google-owned cloud security shop’s pentesting AI agent. The AI systems will uncover public exposures and attack paths across public services, critical infrastructure, and nonprofits, and then hand these off for verification and remediation to human security researchers. “The program has been active over the past several months, and with this official launch, we are scaling it globally,” Gal Nagli, head of offensive security at Wiz, told The Register. “There is no set end date.” It's similar to OpenAI’s Daybreak for Frontline Defenders initiative, announced earlier this month. This program will distribute $1 billion in credits to subsidize access to OpenAI services and training for resource-strapped cyber defenders, including those protecting water and energy systems, community banks, local governments, nonprofits, and open-source projects. And like OpenAI’s new program, the Wiz and Google DeepMind partnership follows disclosures that Google’s AI agents also escaped their sandboxes and hacked other companies’ websites - as did agents developed by OpenAI, Anthropic, and Meta, and those are just the ones we know about. It also comes as existential dread about AI killing all of humanity reaches a fever pitch. Scan for Good aims to put offensive security agents and Gemini 3.8 Flash Cyber to good, not evil, use. When authorized, either explicitly by organizations that apply for an assessment or under applicable bug bounty programs and vulnerability disclosure policies, the AIs will examine publicly facing websites, APIs, and applications for exposures, and then work with organizations to find and fix these. Every potential finding will be reviewed and validated by a human, and Wiz assures that “humans will remain responsible for confirming impact and making disclosure decisions.” When the bots and humans do identify a serious issue, the humans will contact the affected organization and work with them to remediate the security holes. Google’s AI systems have already helped critical organizations and tech providers find serious, internet-facing risks, including a critical GitHub Actions workflow vulnerability in one of Snowflake’s public repositories. In this bug-hunting expedition, conducted through Snowflake’s HackerOne vulnerability disclosure program, Wiz’s Red Agent autonomously identified a script injection vulnerability in snowflakedb/snowflake-connector-net. The flaw allowed an unauthenticated user to execute arbitrary commands within a GitHub Actions runner by opening a GitHub issue with a specially crafted title. Wiz disclosed the issue on June 23, and Snowflake fixed it on the same day, rotated the affected credential, and verified through detailed audit logs that Wiz was the only actor during the exposure window. The Google-owned biz provided several other examples of its AI for good, and said all of these were autonomously discovered by the models before Wiz validated them, but “only far enough to confirm real-world impact.” At that point, they privately notified the affected organization about the issue and helped it remediate the vulnerability.                                                                        Some of these include: An exposed administrator key enabled read, write, and delete access to 8.8 million files in a “nationally significant archive” belonging to an unnamed Middle Eastern country. Assigning the correct set of permissions fixed the flaw. A public hospital with missing access controls exposed staff contact information and gave anyone online control of a hospital-wide mobile alert channel. A private hospital’s public appointment-booking site used an unsafe upload method that would have allowed attackers to take control of a hospital server and obtain patient identifiers, clinical information, and consent signatures. A municipality’s public data service exposed sensitive personal, health, and financial information belonging to about 5,000 elderly residents. Wiz confirmed the risk without collecting a bulk dataset. A public rail operator had a leaky production database that exposed active administrator sessions. This could have allowed criminals to take control of routes, schedules, service announcements, and administrator accounts - essentially disrupting the entire transportation system. Wiz helped the operator secure the system. The US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) also gave Scan for Good its stamp of approval, and Wiz told us the American cyber-defense agency provided guidance on the initiative. “At a time of evolving threats, defensive vulnerability discovery helps strengthen the nation’s digital infrastructure,” CISA acting director Nick Andersen said in a statement. ® Contact details and order records accessed, but PC maker is keeping schtum on how many customers are affected Matt Rosoff chairs a Register roundtable with HPE and NVIDIA on data sovereignty in the AI era, off the record, in Washington PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity A desktop pitch and an attempt to curb LLM slop descended into very human mayhem Kids can learn why BGP matters in a semester, but that won’t leave them ready to implement it Russian devs alleged to have built tools for US agencies as software from same op was sold to Moscow's security services Gemini 3.8 Flash Cyber and Wiz's Red Agent team up to protect hospitals, public transit, and tech Developers test what they can build with TypeSafe's fast, typed decision model Anthropic and OpenAI debut Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Sol and Luna 'first' publicly documented Windows implant to use LLMs for C2 China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' City council may be waking from its Oracle nightmare but the accounting hangover continues
2026-09-24

City council may be waking from its Oracle nightmare but the accounting hangover continues

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Europe's largest local authority still cannot get auditors to vouch for its books as historic data problems linger Europe's largest local authority will go at least one more financial year without auditors being able to vouch for its accounts despite finally reimplementing Oracle Fusion. Grant Thornton expects Birmingham City Council to receive "disclaimer opinions" for 2025/26, 2026/27, and 2027/28 due to problems with historic data. A disclaimer opinion means the auditor cannot obtain enough evidence to express an opinion on the financial statements. It does not mean the council failed to produce accounts, but it leaves stakeholders without independent assurance that those accounts are reliable. A report from the auditor said the council's transition to a rebuilt Oracle Fusion-based HR and finance system in August had been largely successful. It added that the "reimplementation appears to have been better managed than the original 2022 implementation." Council taxpayers might hope so. The first implementation of Oracle Fusion left the council unable to reconcile its bank accounts or reliably understand its cash position, and contributed to the crisis that preceded its effective bankruptcy. The total project, including the reimplementation, is set to cost around £144.4 million against an initial estimate of £20 million. Including forgone efficiency savings, researchers have estimated the cost could reach £216 million. Its early performance contrasts sharply with that of the original system, known as 1B, which went live in April 2022. "There are no outstanding business-critical or priority level 1 or 2 tickets or issues. For context, relating to the old 1B Oracle system in September 2025 there were 311 open tickets and 2 major incidents," the report said. Nonetheless, the council is not expected to regain a clean audit opinion before 2028/29 – roughly a decade after the Oracle program began. The council picked Oracle Fusion to replace its legacy R/3 SAP system and initially planned to go live in December 2020 for finance and procurement, and in February 2021 for HR and payroll. A 2021 business case subsequently moved both launches to April 2022. Although the council had planned to implement Oracle "out of the box," it introduced several customizations, including a bank-reconciliation design that failed to function properly. The council struggled to understand its cash position and could not provide auditors with sufficient evidence to verify its accounts. It has spent more than £5 million on manual workarounds. ® Contact details and order records accessed, but PC maker is keeping schtum on how many customers are affected Matt Rosoff chairs a Register roundtable with HPE and NVIDIA on data sovereignty in the AI era, off the record, in Washington PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity A desktop pitch and an attempt to curb LLM slop descended into very human mayhem Kids can learn why BGP matters in a semester, but that won’t leave them ready to implement it Russian devs alleged to have built tools for US agencies as software from same op was sold to Moscow's security services Gemini 3.8 Flash Cyber and Wiz's Red Agent team up to protect hospitals, public transit, and tech Developers test what they can build with TypeSafe's fast, typed decision model Anthropic and OpenAI debut Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Sol and Luna 'first' publicly documented Windows implant to use LLMs for C2 China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' You might not miss what Nikon removed from its cheaper full-frame Z5 IIC

The Verge | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
The 24.5-megapixel camera ditches some of the Z5 II’s premium features but shaves $450 off the original’s price. The 24.5-megapixel camera ditches some of the Z5 II’s premium features but shaves $450 off the original’s price. Nikon waited five years to update its full-frame Z5 camera to the Z5 II, but today it announced a new variant of last year’s upgrade that removes features instead of introducing improvements in order to make it more affordable for novice photographers. While the Nikon Z5 II starts at $1,849.95, the new Nikon Z5 IIC is $450 cheaper at $1,399.95 with a refreshed design and all of the original’s best core features. The Nikon Z5 IIC carries forward the Z5 II’s 24.5-megapixel backside illuminated full-frame sensor using the same EXPEED 7 image processing engine found in the company’s higher-end shooters with in-body image stabilization. It can capture JPEG images at up to 30 fps using its electronic shutter with the option to capture images for up to a full second before the shutter button is pressed. It also features a mechanical shutter that can shoot up to 15 fps in JPEG mode, which is a feature you won’t find on the new $1,899 full-frame Canon EOS R8 Mark II. To get the price down, the Z5 IIC loses the Z5 II’s electronic viewfinder while the resolution of its 3.2-inch articulated touchscreen has been significantly reduced from 2.1 million to 1.04 million dots. The Z5 IIC also includes just a single UHS-II SD card slot so there’s no redundancy for captured images and videos, and it lacks a joystick requiring you to instead adjust autofocus points and navigate menus using its multi-directional controller. The Verge
Why can’t we just keep rogue AIs off the internet?
2026-09-24

Why can’t we just keep rogue AIs off the internet?

The Verge | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Air-gapping AI to keep it away from real-world targets makes testing safer, but less useful. Air-gapping AI to keep it away from real-world targets makes testing safer, but less useful. AI agents keep getting loose, escaping supposedly secure tests to attack real-world targets, commandeer obscure wikis, and leave instructions for other agents to follow. Researchers are testing these systems precisely because they might behave in unpredictable, even dangerous, ways. So wouldn’t it be safer to just keep the agents off the internet? “A strict air gap reduces realism ... [It’s a] trade-off, not a fundamental technical issue.” In theory, yes. Researchers can isolate the computers running AI tools from the internet and other outside networks, a technique known as air gapping. That can mean physically removing or disabling cables and wireless hardware and using “dumb” peripherals, with particularly sensitive setups using Faraday cages or other shielding to block electromagnetic signals from getting in or out. Done properly, an air-gapped system would offer agents no straightforward route to external targets, or outside systems any straightforward route in, making it much harder, if not impossible, to pull off attacks like the one OpenAI’s models launched against Hugging Face. But in practice, a perfectly sealed box makes for a rather limited laboratory, particularly when the aim is to assess how an AI will perform in the real world. While some AI experiments can be run on air-gapped machines, realistic evaluations often require access to external services, APIs, and digital infrastructure, explained Thorsten Holz, a scientific director at the Max Planck Institute for Security and Privacy in Germany. “A strict air gap reduces realism,” he said, describing the decision to air gap as a “trade-off, not a fundamental technical issue.” Ruizhe Li, an assistant professor in the school of computer science at the University of Birmingham in the UK, likened complete isolation to testing AI in an “artificial vacuum,” potentially undermining the value of the evaluation itself. “We will end up testing a neutered AI model, which blinds evaluators to how the AI model behaves, fails, or executes tool-use exploits in realistic deployment settings,” Li said. Realism isn’t the only tradeoff. Li said air gapping is costly and can slow research to an absolute crawl, turning what would be quick iterations into “a slow logistics hurdle.” Some experiments also become “substantially harder” under a strict air gap, Holz said. That friction may be justified for risky experiments, but applying it for everything would slow down the development of new models, said Maksym Andriushchenko, a principal investigator at the ELLIS Institute Tübingen in Germany. And even if researchers wanted to air gap everything, Andriushchenko questioned whether enough secure infrastructure exists to do it at the scale of frontier AI labs. “This all sounds very sci-fi, but is theoretically possible.” It would not eliminate every risk posed by AI, either. Agents could still compromise systems inside of the isolated environment, Holz said, and could theoretically produce “malicious artifacts that could be dangerous if moved outside.” Moreover, air gapping “does nothing to diagnose or resolve the latent risks waiting inside the model,” Li said. There’s also no guarantee that an air gap would remain completely sealed. Someone from the outside could always breach the gap, as happened with Stuxnet malware — a cyberweapon reportedly developed by Israel and the US to sabotage Iran’s nuclear program — which was transmitted via a USB drive. Information may travel in the other direction, too. Researchers have repeatedly demonstrated ways of turning internal computer components into transmitters, which could be a problem if shielding is not perfect. “This all sounds very sci-fi, but is theoretically possible,” Andriushchenko said. That sort of convoluted escape route has become a focal point for online discussions about whether an advanced AI could escape containment. OpenAI researcher Noam Brown recently ignited the debate by suggesting on X that two air-gapped machines could theoretically communicate by manipulating their CPU temperature and reading the changes. “You could even go as far as to say, ‘Well, we should air gap the computers.’ And I’m not convinced that that would be sufficient,” he said. The idea was met with skepticism, and ridicule, on social media, with more generous critics noting the large gap between such a communication method being possible and a pair of AI systems discovering and exploiting it, particularly as the technique would yield painfully slow data transmission speeds. A sufficiently advanced AI might not need to resort to an elaborate escape route. Humans may become convinced to bridge the gap for it. AI safety researchers have worried about such a possibility for years, and recent incidents have provided concrete evidence that models can engage in attempts at social engineering. “This tradeoff deserves much greater scrutiny, and we have seen how easily things can go wrong.” Air gapping is but one means of safeguarding AI systems. “Relying on isolation as a blanket safety solution creates a false sense of security,” Li said. It should be used alongside other measures, like understanding the inner workings of models, ensuring they are aligned, and guarding against human error, the mundane point of failure behind many recent rogue AI incidents. “In practice, testing exists on a spectrum,” he explained, with the field relying on a “tiered containment model rather than an all-or-nothing approach.” Extreme isolation does have its place, though. Stephen Casper, a computer scientist and assistant professor of public policy at the Harvard Kennedy School, described air gapping as a “great idea” for sensitive systems, pointing to its use in nuclear facilities. While not ruling out the possibility that an advanced AI could find some novel way to escape, Casper said at that point we should probably be more worried about prosaic means of breaking containment, such as compliance failures or human error. Recent incidents raise questions over where AI labs are drawing that line. Many breaches involved models being tested for their cybersecurity abilities, and in many respects they performed exactly as designed. The problem was that they did so outside of the boundaries researchers intended to set. Holz said AI “evaluations often prioritize realism and convenience,” but argued agents explicitly designed for offensive cyber capabilities warrant tighter safeguards, potentially including strong isolation and strict monitoring as a default. “This tradeoff deserves much greater scrutiny, and we have seen how easily things can go wrong,” he said. Google is sending an AI satellite into space next week
2026-09-24

Google is sending an AI satellite into space next week

The Verge | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
It’s the first step toward Google’s goal of handling AI tasks from space. It’s the first step toward Google’s goal of handling AI tasks from space. Google is getting ready to launch a satellite with its AI processors to test how well they perform in space, as reported earlier by The New York Times. The move is part of Google’s Project Suncatcher, the company’s experimental initiative that could eventually put AI data centers into orbit. The satellite, equipped with Google’s Tensor Processing Units, will launch aboard a SpaceX Falcon 9 rocket on October 1st, allowing it to harness the power of the sun in low Earth orbit. As noted by Google, the goal of the mission is to measure how well its TPUs “handle the physical stress of spaceflight and the radiation and thermal extremes of space.” This is just one step toward Google’s goal of putting a constellation of chip-equipped satellites into space — an idea Elon Musk, Jeff Bezos, and former Google CEO Eric Schmidt have also floated as an alternative to data centers on Earth. Google plans to put two satellites into orbit next year. During testing, Google found that its TPUs can withstand high levels of g-force and radiation, but notes that “some things can only be tested in space.” It’s also planning to try out a cooling system that involves heat pipes and radiators while the chips are in orbit. Travis Beals, Project Suncatcher’s senior director of product management, tells the NYT that the chips can only run for about 15 minutes before they’ll need to be turned off to cool down. “Exploring space as a viable location for scalable AI compute won’t happen all at once,” Beals writes in the company’s announcement. “It takes methodical engineering, starting with proving our hardware can handle the physical and unpredictable realities of operating in orbit. This first launch is about seeing what works, identifying points of failure, and applying those findings to future missions.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. The vibes are bad for Flock in Washington
2026-09-24

The vibes are bad for Flock in Washington

The Verge | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
The new topic uniting lawmakers appears to be cracking down on Flock. The new topic uniting lawmakers appears to be cracking down on Flock. Flock is in the hot seat in Washington, even if its CEO declined to actually face senators at a hearing about its “AI Surveillance Network” on Wednesday. “There’s many players in this industry, but there’s one name that really stands out above the rest, and that is Flock,” Senate Judiciary crime and counterterrorism subcommittee chair Josh Hawley (R-MO) said. “If you haven’t heard of Flock, all I have to say is, Flock’s probably heard of you.” Though the CEOs of Flock and peer companies Axon, Motorola Solutions, and Verkada declined to testify, according to Hawley, seats in the hearing room were in high demand. A standby line stretched down the hallway even after they were filled. Instead of the CEOs, the hearing featured testimony from cybersecurity and legal experts, a sheriff whose department ended its Flock contract after allegedly receiving slippery answers about its AI use, and a Florida woman who said she spent 13 days in jail after her car was incorrectly identified by Flock cameras as being connected to a crime scene. Verkada spokesperson Virginia Ramsey said the company provided information to the subcommittee to assist in preparation even though its CEO could not attend. “As we’ve explained to the Subcommittee, none of our customers can search a national Verkada network – because none exists. Our customers can only access the data in their own systems,” Ramsey said. Representatives for the other companies invited did not immediately respond to The Verge’s request for comment. “If you haven’t heard of Flock, all I have to say is, Flock’s probably heard of you” The federal hearing shows just how much the conversation around Flock has escalated, from concerns within individual police departments about reported abuse by cops, to raucous city council meetings and testy state capitol hearings — and now, a Congressional hearing. Less than two months before Election Day for the midterms, lawmakers across the aisle appeared largely aligned and even chummy over their unease about automated license plate readers (ALPRs) that hoover up location data for Americans who haven’t been accused of crimes, and can store that information, in many cases, for an entire month. Lawmakers expressed concern about how securely Flock is able to store its vast trove of images, which cybersecurity engineer Benn Jordan said he was able to access with minimal technical maneuvering, in an incident Flock has said it’s since addressed. Jordan said he’s more recently been granted greater access to Flock to evaluate it, and said its security has improved from a year ago, but broader industry-wide issues persist. The lawmakers also took issue with how easy it appears to be for officers to access Flock data, and said it’s distinct from traditional license plate readers, whose systems don’t construct the same dossier of connected information on individual vehicles. Democrats like Sens. Alex Padilla (D-CA) and Dick Durbin (D-IL) asked how federal immigration authorities could be legally barred from accessing Flock data, and about potential loopholes, which one witness said could be as simple as getting a sympathetic local officer to perform a search on the agency’s behalf. Sen. Cory Booker (D-NJ) asked whether officers in states that restrict abortion access could track people seeking reproductive care across state lines. On the Republican side, Hawley pointed out that similar concerns could apply for gun owners. “For those of us who are supporters of the Second Amendment, we’ve long feared national databases of any kind. You could easily put these cameras up in front of a gun show or near a gun store or a range. You can put them up in front of pregnancy care centers, and you can do all the same things that Senator Booker was talking about,” Hawley said. “We’re all just getting up to speed on this” But it’s still early days for legislating around Flock and other ALPRs. After the hearing, Hawley told reporters that he hadn’t heard of the company until a month or two ago, and “we’re all just getting up to speed on this.” He said he’d still like to hear from the CEOs directly, but subpoenaing them to appear would require a full committee vote, and he hadn’t yet discussed that with Judiciary Committee Chair Chuck Grassley (R-IA). Then there’s the issue of whether President Donald Trump, who recently said “I like them” of the cameras, would support legislation that restrains them. Hawley said he hasn’t asked that yet. “In Missouri, we’ve been using license plate readers forever… but that’s really different than the technology we’re talking about today,” Hawley told reporters. “I think that’s something that I’ve only recently come to appreciate, and I bet most folks are just getting up to speed on. Citizens know though. They know when they’re being tracked. They don’t want to be tracked and treated like criminals. And I think law-abiding citizens just should not be treated like criminals.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. The Verge
I have some questions for Mark Zuckerberg
2026-09-24

I have some questions for Mark Zuckerberg

The Verge | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
What Mark Zuckerberg needs to answer about Meta Glasses, Muse, and teen safety. It’s a big week for Meta. The company just kicked off its big Connect conference on Wednesday, and the new Muse AI agent appears to be an early hit. I’ve been using it — it is surprisingly good. CEO Mark Zuckerberg has obviously been out there taking a victory lap and participating in a lot of interviews about all this. So of course, we asked for him to appear on Decoder. Meta declined my interview request for whatever reason. But luckily Mark just did a big interview with our friend and former colleague Alex Heath on the Sources podcast. So I figure we could just highlight some moments from that conversation and I could point out some obvious gaps in what Mark is saying about the company right now, and hopefully the next set of people who get to talk to him can ask him some follow-up questions. So here’s some of the things I would love to ask Mark Zuckerberg if he would join me here on Decoder. Let’s start with Mark’s comments on the Meta Glasses. Mark Zuckerberg: So we actually feel quite good about the product. I mean, phones don’t have a light, but I mean, people go around recording people all the time. So I think it’s very important to note that the entire tech industry really wants passive surveillance devices to fit into the same category as phones — for you to understand everything from Flock cameras to Ring doorbells to Meta Glasses the same way that you might understand a smartphone. Now, there are some very obvious differences here that I think are worth really pushing on in these conversations. For example, if you want to record someone with your phone, you have to take it out of your pocket, open the camera app, and literally hold it in their face. This is a physical action that is meaningfully different from having a pair of glasses on your face with a light and just passively recording someone. That physical action means that you have to announce your intent to do something with your body and other people can react to it. Obviously, you don’t do that with Meta Glasses. You just start recording and people can notice if there’s a light or not, but the big physical announcement that you’re about to begin recording is not present. So I think one of the reasons the entire tech industry is so eager to fit these passive surveillance devices into the same kind of mental framework as smartphones is so that no one has to question whether there should be a new social etiquette for these devices. Instead, you’re like, “Oh, it’s just a smartphone. I already understand how to use that.” But importantly, that also brushes over the fact that we did have to create a massive new set of social rules and norms around smartphone recording. We did live through the entire Black Lives Matter movement because smartphone recording allowed people to record the police, and we as a society didn’t quite know what to do with that information and maybe still don’t know what to do with that information. I would just push on this over and over and over again. Do you want there to be constant recordings of everyone? Do we think that smartphones allowing everyone to record everything all the time is good? Do we think being able to livestream everything all the time was good? If you could reboot that in a new form factor that allowed for easier and more passive recording, what norms would you change? There’s already examples of this inside of Meta, where they know the Meta glasses are being used to harass retail workers and women, and they have started down-ranking those videos on Instagram to provide less incentive to make them. So I think a version of this question you could ask very directly is if you know some of the content that is being made with these glasses is so bad that you’ve down-ranked it on your own distribution platform, what is so good about these glasses that make it worth having these debates over and over again? All right, let’s get back to Mark’s comments on Meta glasses. Mark Zuckerberg: When we launched the glasses a few years back, we communicated pretty clearly about these steps that we put into it. But like you said, there’s now many, many, many millions of people who’ve gotten the glasses more than had them when we just started launching the product. So I think some of that communication that we did at the beginning, a lot of people either maybe- Mark Zuckerberg: Or they forgot about it or they didn’t see at the beginning or they just weren’t paying attention to it because the glasses weren’t a big thing. Now that I think we’ve achieved a level of mainstream… well, at a minimum, I think we need to go and make sure that we communicate about what we’re doing and that, yes, we think that this is important and, in fact, so important that we designed it into the product from the very first version that we shipped multiple years ago. All right, let’s stop here. So I would ask some very simple questions here. What is important? Is it the privacy of the user? Is it the privacy of the person who might be being recorded? And what is it that you thought you were communicating clearly at the beginning that got lost? What I think Mark is trying to say is at the very beginning, they announced that the glasses would have a light, that the light would be on when you’re recording, that they thought it was obvious that there was a camera in the temple. Over time, as more people got the glasses and more people experienced the glasses on other people’s faces, it was not clear what that light was or what it meant. Now, Mark thinks they have to do more communication about specifically the light existing. My response to all of that is it doesn’t matter what Meta says. This is the essential truth of every piece of mainstream consumer technology. The users define the product. If you sell millions of gadgets to people, they will use them in ways you never expected. It’s those emergent behaviors that often define the next version of the product. Probably one of the most famous examples of this is Twitter. Twitter didn’t invent the hashtag or the @ reply or any of a million features that we associate with Twitter. Users on Twitter invented those features, and Twitter adopted them because its users had figured out a way to use the service in ways that it had never even contemplated. This is broadly true of everything. It’s true of computers, it’s true of smartphones, it’s true of social platforms, and it is true of Meta Glasses. The ways people are using them have nothing to do with Meta’s intentions or its communication about recording lights at the beginning or now. So I think the natural follow-up question we should all be asking is how much control does Meta have over bad actors who are using these glasses to harass people? Because just telling more people that the light indicates recording isn’t going to stop it. Are they going to do more things like down-ranking videos of people harassing retail workers? Do they think the other platforms will follow their lead? Does Meta think that creating negative incentives for how people use a camera with content moderation will actually solve the problem? Is there anything about the history of content moderation that suggests that that’s true, given Mark’s own history with content moderation? Next up, Mark talks about teen safety and Meta’s recent multi-billion dollar teen safety settlement. Mark Zuckerberg: I think it would’ve been possible to have explained along the way how seriously we took those issues, and we just didn’t because we thought, well, people are showing that by using the product so much they still like the products. Okay, let me stop there. The claim in many of the social media lawsuits is that the product is addictive. The reason people keep comparing social media to Big Tobacco is that smoking is fun and people enjoyed it, and it was killing them. So the usage rates had nothing to do with the harm that might’ve been occurring. So from the jump here, I would’ve wanted to push on whether simply measuring usage rates actually lets you measure whether or not something is doing good for the world or good for an individual user. Mark Zuckerberg: Where we’ve gotten with the social media products, which is both people liking it and understanding how seriously we take all of those issues. So that’s what I aspire to. Alex Heath: Is there a throughline from that to the recent settlement on all the use safety stuff? I know it’s a thing a lot of people are talking about. Is there any connection from what you just said to that? I would just be curious to hear you reflect on that and what you’ve learned from this process. Mark Zuckerberg: Yeah, no, I think it’s another good example like this. I mean, we’ve taken a lot of those safety issues seriously for a while and we’ve been working on this teen account work with Instagram for a long time. So it is true that Meta has done some of the leading work on designing features that would keep kids safer on its platforms. They’ve done some of the leading research in the space on what is actually happening to people on their platforms. And yet one of the dynamics that is particularly galling throughout all of this is that Meta gets punished for it. So they publish their safety numbers, they publish their research, they publish the amount of bad things that are happening, they do transparency reports. Even if you don’t think that that’s enough, a real thing that continually happens to Meta is they get punished for revealing that bad things happen on their platforms. Companies like Apple, which don’t know what bad things are happening on an encrypted service like iCloud, don’t publish those numbers and don’t get punished for it. What I would’ve asked Mark here is: How do you solve that dynamic specifically? Because in these lawsuits, it’s been revealed in internal emails that Mark himself has questioned the value of doing the research and building the features because Meta gets punished for it. So there’s a really strong negative feedback loop here where Meta tries hard and gets punished for it because they’re transparent about it, but in fact, you would want a government regulator to solve it. If all the social media companies were required to be transparent about various metrics around teen safety, then Meta would be judged in comparison to its peers, not for the information existing at all. That doesn’t appear in this settlement. Instead, what we have is a bunch of behavior controls, product restrictions. Meta seems very keen on making sure those product restrictions connect to TikTok and YouTube, but not on whether we should be able to measure the harms themselves. So that’s the first thing that I think is worth pushing on: Does this whole industry need to be required to be more transparent about the things happening to children on their platforms? Because if you’re going to make rules and regulations, you should probably start from a common set of information. Mark Zuckerberg: If you basically said, “Limit usage to an hour a day for teens,” everyone, I think, would be okay with that, except you have to unilaterally do it. If not, then you’re saying, “Okay, if people don’t use Instagram for more than an hour a day, but then their usage goes to TikTok, have we really helped anyone and we’ve just hurt ourselves to not help anyone?” So what Mark is describing here is a prisoner’s dilemma, where if they take action to make teens safer and TikTok doesn’t, the teens will just go use TikTok and no one will be safe. This idea, of course, is going to come up again when we talk about AI safety, but let’s just talk about social platforms for one minute. If you believe that there’s a market for safety, if your initial premise was people are using the platform so they must be happy with it, well, then you should make the platform safer for kids, and parents might say to their children, “You’re allowed to use Instagram and not TikTok because Instagram has these controls.” It is very telling to me that Mark doesn’t think there’s a market for safety, that instead he thinks that everyone must be forced to unilaterally disarm by the government in a regulatory framework that solves his prisoner’s dilemma. So my big question here is: Why is this a problem for the government to solve in social media in a way that might implicate the First Amendment and the speech rights of teenagers, but not a problem for the government to solve in AI safety, where the frontier labs keep talking about killing everyone? By the way, when I say this connects directly to AI safety, what I mean is that Mark is arguing the opposite side of the prisoner’s dilemma when it comes to AI safety. After all the calls for slowdowns from the various frontier labs, Mark posted a thread on X saying he thought the market would take care of it. I’ll just read it to you. “My view is that trust and alignment are quickly becoming the most important capabilities that will differentiate agents and models. Any lab that doesn’t focus on alignment will fall behind. Labs face significant liability if their models cause harm, so they have a strong incentive to prevent this as well. Meta delayed shipping Muse for several months to focus on safety and security. We didn’t call for everyone else to do this before we would. We just did it as part of our day-to-day work because it was clearly the right thing for people and for us. I’m proud of the security foundations we’ve built.” So straightforwardly, I think the question is why is the go-it-alone approach appropriate when it comes to AI safety, but getting the government to pull the other social media companies into a framework for teen safety appropriate in that market? The other question I’m just going to steal from my friend Casey Newton, who pointed out that in the most recent teen safety case, Meta estimated its own liability at a trillion dollars in damages, which does not appear to be enough liability for Meta to take unilateral action to prevent teens from being harmed on its platforms. If a trillion dollars in potential liability isn’t enough, how much is enough for Meta to take AI safety seriously? And is there any number in a liability scheme that would stop everyone all at once? Mark then got into how he plans to run Muse as a business. Mark Zuckerberg: It, I think, is just going to be able to make people money and save people money… the reason for why we’re doing this is we basically are confident in standing behind the fact that we think that this is going to effectively, for people who are going to use it for running a small business or making money or transactions or commerce in some way, we actually just think it’s going to make so much money for people that the business model over time that we expect is to effectively just take a very small cut of whatever the transaction is. Mark Zuckerberg: Yeah, and not even necessarily the person paying for it. It’ll come from the businesses that they’re working with. So if you’re a Ben Thompson fan or you’ve just listened to enough Decoder, you know that what Zuck is describing here is aggregation theory. He’s going to have all the consumer demand, and it’ll be so big and so lucrative that businesses who want customers will pay Meta a small fee to be on the other side of transactions that happen in Muse. Being one of the big aggregators is a very lucrative thing. All the big tech giants aggregate demand in one way or another. The problem is the businesses on the other side of the aggregators hate them. Mark knows this better than most because he’s on the other side of one of the biggest aggregators of all — Apple’s App Store. If you want to reach customers on iPhones, you have to go through the Apple App Store and, importantly, agree to a bunch of privacy rules and pay 30 percent fees on most of the transactions that happen on an iPhone. This hits Meta in two ways. The first is that Apple nearly killed Meta’s ad business with something called App Tracking Transparency when they issued a rule change that really favored Apple’s ad businesses over Meta’s. And two, if you want to boost a post on Instagram from your phone, well, you basically have to pay Apple 30 percent, a fee that Meta breaks out. Mark hates this so much that he has explicitly talked about building new platforms to get away from Apple for years now. One of the reasons he chased the Metaverse was to build the platform that came after the iPhone and escape Apple. One of the reasons he’s chasing Meta glasses is to build the platform that comes after the iPhone and get away from Apple. And I am dead certain one of the reasons he’s invested so heavily into AI and now Muse is to build the platform that comes after the iPhone and get away from Apple So the question I would ask Mark here is very simple: How will you make it so all of the businesses downstream of your huge aggregator AI agent feel good about this arrangement? Because when you’ve been in this arrangement, you have felt very, very bad. Mark also spent some time talking about Meta’s data center investments. Mark Zuckerberg: So if you look at the data centers, what we’ve actually found is that when a company like Meta goes into a community and makes a commitment that we’re going to invest there for decades, which is really what we’re doing when we’re building up a data center, we’re able to make it so that it’s very good for the community. I mean, the tax revenue that they bring from that. I mean, in Louisiana, we have this example where the tax revenue funded these $50,000 bonuses for teachers in the community. Wait, hold on. All right, so this data center in Louisiana, Hyperion… Meta is obviously very proud of it. Mark is talking about the positive impacts it might have for that community. But the actual process of landing that data center deal was basically a bunch of secrecy and backroom deals and huge asks for property tax exemptions from the state government that residents really didn’t know about. There’s a huge New York Times piece about this project that really goes into detail about the kinds of backroom negotiations that Meta secured in order to go fast and build this data center. So I would simply ask if companies like Meta should lay out these supposed benefits in a much more public and transparent way and let people actually decide for themselves. Mark Zuckerberg: Of course, you have to design it in a way that can be helpful to people in all these ways, which is also part of the reason why, for people who are skeptical or have so much doom about the whole thing, I mean, one of the things that I basically think is that if we don’t end up building it in a way that’s positive, it just effectively won’t be able to happen. This is another market argument from Zuck. He’s saying if people don’t like the AI that’s being built or find it helpful, it simply won’t be allowed to exist. The market will kill the products. You can feel a lot of different ways about that, especially if you’ve experienced AI being force-fed to you in your apps and services right now, but I would connect that to social media, where a lot of people felt that social media was doing harm and the industry raced ahead anyway until the government required some regulations. So I’m once again very curious about Mark’s different postures to government regulation market forces when it comes to social media, where he thinks there should be government regulation, and AI, where he thinks the market should be able to run however it wants. Secondly, and I think most importantly, I’ve spent a lot of time on this show asking various AI executives if consumer AI products are good enough to overcome doomerism and skepticism. Because right now, they’re not. Even the way Mark describes Muse as being helpful for people running businesses or helping people save money or doing shopping, there’s fundamentally enterprise applications. They’re not big consumer applications that make people overcome their skepticism. For example, Instagram is a beloved consumer application. It just entertains you. That means all of the downstream harms of Instagram fade away because people like being entertained. It is not clear to me whether people are going to love letting Muse run their small businesses so much that they will accept data centers in their community. It’s not clear to me what consumer AI products will ever exist such that people overcome their skepticism of the enormous cost of data center build-outs. I would want to know if Mark thinks Muse is that great consumer product, or if there’s something yet to come. So those are some of the questions I would’ve asked Mark Zuckerberg if he had agreed to come on Decoder. Questions or comments? Hit us up at decoder@theverge.com. We really do read every email! Waymo’s driverless cars continue to crash less often than people
2026-09-24

Waymo’s driverless cars continue to crash less often than people

The Verge | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Waymo says after 271 million driverless miles, its vehicles were involved in 82 percent fewer injury-causing crashes as compared to human drivers. Waymo says after 271 million driverless miles, its vehicles were involved in 82 percent fewer injury-causing crashes as compared to human drivers. Waymo’s latest batch of self-reported data continues to affirm the company’s message that its driverless cars are safer than human drivers. As policymakers continue to debate the safety and economic impact of autonomous vehicles, Waymo is hoping that its own data will help bring more supporters to its side — even as some experts say the data fails to capture the non-crash traffic disruptions caused by its robotaxis. Waymo said it examined 271 million driverless miles in five cities through the end of June, which is 50 million more miles than its last safety report. The total number of miles driven across Atlanta, Austin, Phoenix, Los Angeles, and the San Francisco-Bay Area is the equivalent of over “300 human lifetimes behind the wheel,” assuming every human drives the equivalent of 850,000 miles in their lifetime. The company plans to update its online safety hub to reflect the new driving figures. Waymo’s mileage seems like a lot, but its still a drop in the bucket compared to the 3 trillion miles driven by humans in the US every year. So there’s still a pretty big gap when trying to draw comparisons between robot driven vehicles and humans. While Waymo’s data only covers five cities, the company is operating nearly 4,000 vehicles across more than 10 US cities, logging roughly two million driverless miles per week while delivering over 500,000 paid rides weekly. But some cities, including big ones like New York, Boston, and Washington, DC, are still reluctant to let Waymo open up shop, citing concerns about traffic disruptions and job losses. Waymo is hoping that by continuing to show that its technology can drastically cut down on injuries and fatalities on roads, it can help persuade those officials who remain unconvinced. But don’t just take Waymo’s word for it. A recent study by the Insurance Institute for Highway Safety found that per vehicle mile traveled, Waymo’s driverless vehicles were involved in 68 percent fewer crashes than human drivers. But the researchers warned that the present data collection system “isn’t good enough to allow continuous monitoring of a large-scale expansion,” IIHS President David Harkey said in a statement. Indeed, there’s some concern about relying on Waymo’s self-reported data to assess the safety of its autonomous vehicles. Experts from institutions like Johns Hopkins University point out that a lack of uniform guidelines across the industry makes it hard to compare data apples to apples. Critics also note that comparing expensive, sensor-laden robotaxis against average human-driven vehicles —which often include older cars or distracted drivers — mostly proves that advanced emergency braking and sensors work, rather than explicitly validating autonomous software superiority. And safety experts like Carnegie Mellon’s Phil Koopman argue that aggregate safety dashboards like Waymo’s can be used as corporate PR, downplaying real-world edge cases, embarrassing traffic jams, and minor incidents. In addition to reporting its own data, Waymo is also required to report crashes — defined as physical contact with another vehicle, person, or property — to the federal government under a 2021 standing general order. According to the data collected under that order, Waymo’s vehicles were involved in 797 crashes across all cities March-September 2026. The company reported one fatality, in which a pedestrian in Dallas was struck by a human driver and then ricocheted off the pavement and into a Waymo.
